Classification and enumeration of solid-solid phase transition mechanisms
Fang-Cheng Wang, Qi-Jun Ye, Yu-Cheng Zhu, Xin-Zheng Li

Abstract
Crystal-structure match (CSM), the atom-to-atom correspondence between two crystalline phases, is used extensively to describe solid-solid phase transition (SSPT) mechanisms. However, existing computational methods cannot account for all possible CSMs. Here, we propose a formalism to classify all CSMs into a tree structure, which is independent of the choices of unit cell and supercell. We rigorously proved that only a finite number of noncongruent CSMs are of practical interest. By representing CSMs as integer matrices, we introduce the crystmatch method to exhaustively enumerate them, which uncontroversially solves the CSM optimization problem under any geometric criterion. For most SSPTs, crystmatch can reproduce all known deformation mechanisms and CSMs within 10 CPU minutes, while also revealing thousands of new candidates. The resulting database can be further used for comparing experimental phenomena, high-throughput energy barrier calculations, or machine learning.



I
Introduction

In solid-solid phase transition (SSPT), atoms in one crystal structure are rearranged to form another crystal structure.
This process establishes an atom-to-atom correspondence between the initial and final structures, which is called a crystal-structure match (CSM)
Stevanović et al. 2018
;
Therrien et al. 2020
;
Wang et al. 2024
.
Every transition path on the potential energy surface (PES) determines a CSM, while the latter describes the SSPT more concisely and flexibly.
Consequently, it is common for researchers to identify the CSM, rather than the transition path, to determine which SSPT mechanism is involved
Zahn et al. 2005
;
Shimojo et al. 2004
;
Cai and Chen 2007
;
Zahn and Leoni 2004
;
Badin and Martoňák 2021
.
This is especially true in nucleation simulations
Zahn and Leoni 2004
;
Badin and Martoňák 2021
;
Santos-Florez et al. 2022
;
Khaliullin et al. 2011
, where the term “mechanism” is almost synonymous with “CSM”, which serves as a common language for both nucleation and concerted mechanism studies
Wang et al. 2024
.

It has long been believed that SSPTs, like chemical reactions, occur along a minimum energy path (MEP) with the lowest energy barrier
Eyring 1935
;
Sheppard et al. 2012
;
Therrien et al. 2021
.
Just as each transition path determines a CSM, each CSM can also yield an MEP through methods such as the solid-state nudged elastic band (NEB)
Sheppard et al. 2012
.
However, NEB-like methods can only produce an MEP with the user-specified CSM, ignoring all other CSMs and their MEPs, as illustrated in Fig.
1
.
Hence, despite their great success, NEB-like methods may still fail to give the lowest-barrier MEP, which has been an open question in the SSPT mechanism research for the past decade
Therrien et al. 2021
;
Zhu et al. 2019
;
Shang et al. 2014
.
The main difficulty is that unlike the atom-to-atom correspondence in a chemical reaction, possible CSMs in an SSPT are extremely numerous—due to the large number of identical atoms and possible ways of lattice deformation—and thus hard to determine by human intuition
Stevanović et al. 2018
;
Therrien et al. 2020
;
Wang et al. 2024
.

To investigate what CSMs are favored by the SSPT, efforts have been made on two fronts: variable-CSM MEP calculation
Shang et al. 2014
;
Zhu et al. 2019
and pure geometric CSM optimization
Therrien et al. 2021
.
The former includes the stochastic surface walking (SSW)
Shang et al. 2014
;
Guan et al. 2015
;
Zhang and Liu 2015
and
pallas

Zhu et al. 2019
, where intensive energy calculations hinder the enumeration of CSMs.
The latter relies on a specific optimization criterion, such as minimum strain
Chen et al. 2016
, minimum number of broken bonds
Stevanović et al. 2018
, or minimum total distance traveled by the atoms
Therrien et al. 2020
;
Huang et al. 2024
.
However, geometrically optimized CSMs may not have the lowest energy barrier, and some are even suboptimal under their own criteria
Wang et al. 2024
.
It would be useful to list
all
possible CSMs, both to guide MEP calculations and to identify the best CSM under
any
geometric criterion—yet, such a methodology is lacking.

Figure 1:
A schematic diagram showing that the initial structure (orthorhombic, yellow) may evolve to the final structure (hexagonal, other colors) in a number of ways.
(a)
After specifying the final structure as shown in the blue box, the NEB method can find the MEP denoted by the blue dashed line.
However, to become the final structure, neither the lattice nor the atoms must end up as the blue box.
Other possible endpoints spread out in both lattice and atomic degrees of freedom (DOF) on the PES, whose MEPs (gray dashed lines) are inaccessible.
(b) Endpoints that differ in rigid transformations (cyan) can be easily taken into account by the NEB method
Field 1999
.
(c) Endpoints that differ in CSMs, including those with the same SLM (pink) and different SLMs (purple), which we use
crystmatch
to exhaust.

In this paper, we present a complete classification of CSMs, and the
crystmatch
method for
exhaustively
enumerating them.
To this end, a mathematical formalism is developed in Section
II
such that the deformation gradient, sublattice match (SLM), multiplicity and shuffle distance of a CSM can be rigorously defined in a
cell-independent
manner.
These concepts organize all CSMs into a tree-like structure, as shown in Fig.
2
, whose matrix representation and pruning scheme are discussed in Section
III
.
To traverse this tree, three algorithms are detailed in Section
IV
.
We demonstrate their application using the B1–B2 and graphite-to-diamond transitions as examples in Section
V
.

Figure 2:
Given the initial and final crystal structures, all CSMs between them can be classified into a tree by their multiplicities and SLMs.
Without restrictions, the tree would have infinite breadth at each level.
For empirical (see Table
2
) and computational considerations, an upper bound
\mu_{\text{max}}
for the multiplicity
\mu
is introduced.
Also, CSMs with too much strain or shuffle distance are deemed nonphysical.
Under these prunings, the remaining tree contains only a finite number of CSMs, which can be obtained via Algorithms
1
and
2
.

It should be noted that the molecular dynamics (MD) has also been used extensively to study SSPTs
Scandolo et al. 1995
.
Among different transition paths, the one with the lowest energy barrier is most likely to be obtained by MD.
This bypasses the CSM problem, but it takes an average time
\bar{t}\propto\exp(\beta\Delta^{\ddagger})
to see a rare event with energy barrier
\Delta^{\ddagger}
to happen.
Such
\Delta^{\ddagger}
increases linearly with the supercell volume until it can accommodate a critical nucleus
Sheppard et al. 2012
;
Khaliullin et al. 2011
;
Badin and Martoňák 2021
;
Santos-Florez et al. 2022
, making the MD simulation very hard—if not infeasible.
Fortunately, enhanced sampling techniques like the metadynamics (MetaD)
Laio and Parrinello 2002
;
Martoňák et al. 2003
can reinforce the occurrence of rare events by adding a history-dependent bias potential, making it possible to simulate nucleation in SSPTs
Badin and Martoňák 2021
;
Santos-Florez et al. 2022
.
Nevertheless, the bias potential may elevate different energy barriers
unequally
, thus altering the lowest-barrier path and CSM.
We do not discuss here how
crystmatch
can serve MetaD, but simply show that the enumeration results of the former contain the CSM yielded by the latter.

Figure 3:
Four CSMs from
\mathcal{A}
(orthorhombic) to
\mathcal{B}
(hexagonal).
Each CSM is described by the correspondences between the initial and final supercell vectors (pink), and two translational-inequivalent atoms (black).
From the labels on the atoms (
0-9
and
A-F
) in
\mathcal{B}
, one can see that
\mathcal{J}_{1}=\mathcal{J}_{2}\neq\mathcal{J}_{3}\neq\mathcal{J}_{4}
.

II
Cell-independent formalism

Conventionally, CSMs are described by a pair of initial and final supercells, as shown in Fig.
3
.
However, the multivaluedness of this description causes severe inconveniences for both the theory and the enumeration of CSMs
Wang et al. 2024
;
Therrien et al. 2020
;
Li and Liu 2022
.
The purpose of this section is to establish a
cell-independent
formalism, making it self-evident that multiplicity and SLM—the main subjects in Fig.
2
—are general, singlevalued, intrinsic properties of CSMs.
We also define congruence relations among CSMs and SLMs, which are crucial for the efficiency of
crystmatch
.

To ensure the unambiguousness of the narration and proofs, we define fundamental terms (e.g., “lattice” and “crystal structure”) using the language of set theory.
All lemmas are proved in Appendix
A
, while frequently used symbols are summarized in Table
1
.
Commutative diagrams, as a tool for depicting the composition of homomorphisms, are also adopted to simplify the exposition.
Let us begin with a set of points in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where each point is endowed with an atomic species; we denote by
\mathbb{X}
the set of all atomic species.

Definition 1 (Atomic Structure).

An atomic structure is a nonempty set
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
together with a mapping
\chi_{A}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{X}
.

Definition 2 (Atom-to-Atom Correspondence).

Let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be atomic structures.
We say that a mapping
\mathcal{J}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is an atom-to-atom correspondence if
(1)
\mathcal{J}
is bijective, and
(2)
\mathcal{J}
preserves atom species, i.e., makes the following diagram commute:

(1)

The above diagram is essentially a directed graph, whose vertices are sets and arrows are mappings.
Paths composed of multiple arrows naturally represent a composite mapping, from its starting point to its endpoint.
Commutativity of Eq. (
1
) means that any two paths with the same starting point and endpoint are equal, i.e.,

\chi_{A}=\chi_{B}\circ\mathcal{J}.

(2)

One can also interpret Eq. (
1
) as follows: For any
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
, it is mapped along different paths to

\chi_{A}(\mathbf{a})=\chi_{B}(\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a})),

(3)

which means that the species of atom
\mathbf{a}
is the same as that of its counterpart
\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a})
.

Commutative diagrams are primarily used in category theory and homological algebra, where the arrows represent homomorphisms.
In this spirit, we redefine for all subsequent diagrams:
Arrows between two atomic structures
always
represent atom-to-atom correspondences, i.e., species-preserving bijections.
Consequently, the symbols
\chi_{A}
and
\mathbb{X}
no longer appear in most diagrams.
This approach handles polyatomic SSPTs once and for all.
We hope the reader keeps in mind that
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
denotes an “isomorphism” between atomic structures.

.

II.1
Lattices and crystal structures

Definition 3 (Lattice).

We say that a vector set
L\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a lattice if it (1) is a group under vector addition, (2) is full rank (has three linealy independent elements), and (3) has a positive lower bound
\lambda
on the distance between any two of its elements, i.e.,

\forall\mathbf{t}_{1},\mathbf{t}_{2}\in L,\quad\mathbf{t}_{1}\neq\mathbf{t}_{2}\implies\left|\mathbf{t}_{1}-\mathbf{t}_{2}\right|\geq\lambda.

(4)

Definition 4 (Crystal Structure).

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an atomic structure.
We say that a vector
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a translation element of
\mathcal{A}
if the mapping
\mathbf{a}\mapsto\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{t}
is an atom-to-atom correspondence from
\mathcal{A}
to itself, i.e.,

\hbox to76.22pt{\vbox to15.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 38.11107pt\lower-7.07639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-38.11107pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -16.33 -1.27 L 15.77 -1.27}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{11.6pt}{-0.91666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.83751pt}{2.26945pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(5)

We say that an atomic structure
\mathcal{A}
is a crystal structure if all its translation elements form a lattice, denoted as
L_{A}
.
We refer to
L_{A}
as the lattice of
\mathcal{A}
, and sublattices of
L_{A}
sublattices of
\mathcal{A}
.

Let
\tilde{L}_{A}
be a sublattice of a crystal structure
\mathcal{A}
.
The relation
\sim
on
\mathcal{A}
defined as

\mathbf{a}\sim\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\quad\iff\quad\exists\mathbf{t}\in\tilde{L}_{A},\quad\mathbf{a}^{\prime}=\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{t}

(6)

is an equivalence relation, whose reflexivity, symmetry, and transitivity are ensured by the identity element, invertibility, and closure of the group
(\tilde{L}_{A},+)
, respectively.
We say that
\mathbf{a},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
are
\tilde{L}_{A}
-equivalent, and denote the
\tilde{L}_{A}
-equivalence class of
\mathbf{a}
by

\mathbf{a}+\tilde{L}_{A}=\left\{\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{t}\,\middle|\,\mathbf{t}\in\tilde{L}_{A}\right\},

(7)

and the quotient set by

\mathcal{A}/\tilde{L}_{A}=\left\{\mathbf{a}+\tilde{L}_{A}\,\middle|\,\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}\right\}.

(8)

For any crystal structure
\mathcal{A}
, we denote
Z_{A}=\lvert\mathcal{A}/L_{A}\rvert
, which equals to the number of atoms in a primitive cell.
Actual crystal structures all have a finite
Z_{A}
since they have a finite atomic density.
We will assume this henceforth, but the reader can also modify Definition
1
to make it rigorous; see Lemma
9
.

Definition 5 (CSM).

A crystal structure match (CSM) is an atom-to-atom correspondence between two crystal structures.
We denote the set of all CSMs from crystal structure
\mathcal{A}
to crystal structure
\mathcal{B}
by
\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
.

II.2
Decomposing crystal-structure matches

Now we aim at defining the deformation gradient of a CSM.
Our approach is to first define the
shuffle
, a type of CSM that is considered to have no deformation.
Then, we will define the deformation of a general CSM as the difference between that CSM and a shuffle, as shown in Fig.
4
(a).
In the following text, the composition of mappings (including CSMs, linear transformations, and translations) will appear more frequently.
When there is no risk of confusion, we will denote
f\circ g
simply as
fg
.

(9)

i.e.,
\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{t})=\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a})+\mathbf{t}
for all
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
.
If all translation elements of
\mathcal{J}
form a lattice, we refer to it as the shuffle lattice of
\mathcal{J}
and say that
\mathcal{J}
is a shuffle.
We use a squiggly arrow
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
to denote a shuffle.

In actual SSPTs,
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
in different phases generally have no common translation elements (except
\mathbf{0}
), in which case
\mathcal{J}\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
can never be a shuffle.
However, it is possible to deform
\mathcal{A}
such that the deformed structure shares more translation elements with
\mathcal{B}
, making the deformed CSM a shuffle.
To formalize this idea, we denote by
S\mathcal{A}=\left\{S\mathbf{a}\,\middle|\,\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}\right\}
the crystal structure deformed from
\mathcal{A}
via
S\in\operatorname{GL}(3,\mathbb{R})
.
It has a canonical atom-species mapping
\chi_{A}S^{-1}
, which is the only mapping that makes the following diagram commutes:

4

Figure 4:
(a) An example of
\mathcal{J}\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
being decomposed into a deformation gradient
S\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},S\mathcal{A})
and a shuffle
\mathcal{J}S^{-1}\in\operatorname{CSM}(S\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
. (b) In the above decomposition, a sublattice
\tilde{L}_{A}\subset L_{A}
is deformed to the shuffle lattice
\tilde{L}_{B}\subset L_{B}
. We call the triplet
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
the SLM of
\mathcal{J}
.

(11)

i.e.,
\mathcal{J}S^{-1}\colon S\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is a shuffle.

II.3
Classifying crystal-structure matches

Next, we will show the uniqueness of the decomposition in Fig.
4
(a).
This makes the concept of SLM well-defined, which is crucial for classifying and representing CSMs, as shown in Fig.
4
(b).

Theorem 1.

The deformation gradient of a CSM is either unique or nonexistent.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathcal{J}\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
has two deformation gradients
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
.

7
we have the commutative diagram:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(15)

The commutativity of Eq. (
15
) means that
S_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{t}=S_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{t}
.
By Definitions
6
, both
\tilde{L}_{1}
and
\tilde{L}_{2}
are sublattices of
L_{B}
so that
\tilde{L}_{1}\cap\tilde{L}_{2}
is full rank (Lemma
10
).
Hence, there exist three linearly independent vectors
\mathbf{t}_{1},\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{3}
such that

S_{1}^{-1}[\mathbf{t}_{1},\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{3}]=S_{2}^{-1}[\mathbf{t}_{1},\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{3}].

(16)

Since
[\mathbf{t}_{1},\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{3}]
is invertible, we have
S_{1}=S_{2}
.
∎

Theorem
1
enables us to classify CSMs by their deformation and shuffle.
Now, we merge the deformation gradient and the shuffle lattice into a single concept, whose utility will become evident in Sections
III
and
IV
.

Definition 8 (SLM).

Let
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
be crystal structures,
\tilde{L}_{A}
,
\tilde{L}_{B}
their respective sublattices, and
S\in\operatorname{GL}(3,\mathbb{R})
.
We say that
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
is a sublattice match (SLM) from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
if

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{A}/\tilde{L}_{A}\rvert

\displaystyle=\lvert\mathcal{B}/\tilde{L}_{B}\rvert,

(17)

\displaystyle SL_{A}

\displaystyle=L_{B}.

(18)

We refer to the value of Eq. (
17
) as the period of the SLM, denoted by
\tilde{Z}
, and say that the SLM has multiplicity

\mu=\frac{\tilde{Z}}{\operatorname{lcm}(Z_{A},Z_{B})}.

(19)

Denote by
\operatorname{SLM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
the set of all SLMs from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
, and those with multiplicity
\mu
by
\operatorname{SLM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};\mu)
.

Theorem 2.

If
\mathcal{J}\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
has a deformation gradient
S
, then
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)\in\operatorname{SLM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
, where
\tilde{L}_{B}
is the shuffle lattice of
\mathcal{J}S^{-1}
and
\tilde{L}_{A}=S^{-1}\tilde{L}_{B}
.

Proof.

Eq. (
18
) is already satisfied and we only need to show Eq. (
17
).
Consider a mapping
p\colon\mathcal{A}/\tilde{L}_{A}\to\mathcal{B}/\tilde{L}_{B}
such that the following diagram commutes:

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.03738pt}{-21.44305pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

(20)

i.e.,
p\colon\mathbf{a}+\tilde{L}_{A}\mapsto\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a})+\tilde{L}_{B}
.
To show that
p
is well-defined, we must prove its singlevaluedness.
For any
\mathbf{a}_{1}
and
\mathbf{a}_{2}
in
\mathcal{A}
, we have

\displaystyle\quad\mathbf{a}_{1}+\tilde{L}_{A}=\mathbf{a}_{2}+\tilde{L}_{A}

(21)

\displaystyle\iff{}

\displaystyle\quad\exists\mathbf{t}\in\tilde{L}_{A},\quad\mathbf{a}_{1}=\mathbf{a}_{2}+\mathbf{t}

(22)

\displaystyle\iff{}

\displaystyle\quad\exists\mathbf{t}\in\tilde{L}_{A},\quad S\mathbf{a}_{1}=S\mathbf{a}_{2}+S\mathbf{t}

(23)

\displaystyle\iff{}

\displaystyle\quad\exists\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\in\tilde{L}_{B},\quad S\mathbf{a}_{1}=S\mathbf{a}_{2}+\mathbf{t}^{\prime}

(24)

\displaystyle\iff{}

\displaystyle\quad\exists\mathbf{t}^{\prime}\in\tilde{L}_{B},\quad\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a}_{1})=\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a}_{2})+\mathbf{t}^{\prime}

(25)

\displaystyle\iff{}

\displaystyle\quad\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a}_{1})+\tilde{L}_{B}=\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a}_{2})+\tilde{L}_{B},

(26)

where the shuffle property of
\mathcal{J}S^{-1}\colon S\mathcal{A}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{B}
is used to derive Eq. (
25
).
The equivalence of Eqs. (
21
) and (
26
) means that
p
is injective, while
p
is surjective since
\mathcal{J}
is surjective.
So far, we can see that
p
is bijective and thus Eq. (
17
) holds.
∎

Figure 5:
An example of
R_{A}
(a rotation by
90^{\circ}
) and
R_{B}
(a rotation by
60^{\circ}
) being symmetry operations of
\mathcal{A}
(tetragonal) and
\mathcal{B}
(hexagonal), respectively.
For each
\mathcal{J}\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
, there exists a congruent
R_{B}\mathcal{J}R_{A}^{-1}\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
.
Their respective SLMs are
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
and
(R_{A}\tilde{L}_{A},R_{B}\tilde{L}_{B},R_{B}SR_{A}^{-1})
.

Definition 9 (Multiplicity).

If
\mathcal{J}\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
has a deformation gradient, we say that the
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
defined in Theorem
2
is the SLM of
\mathcal{J}
.
In such case, we refer to the period and multiplicity of the SLM as those of
\mathcal{J}
.
If the deformation gradient of
\mathcal{J}
does not exist, we say that the period and multiplicity of
\mathcal{J}
are
\infty
.

The multiplicity
\mu
defined in Eq. (
19
) is always a positive integer, since the period
\tilde{Z}
is a common multiple of
Z_{A}
and
Z_{B}
(Lemma
4
).
The value of
\mu
and
\tilde{Z}
reflect the intricacy of the CSM—the size of the
smallest
supercell required to describe it.
It must be emphasized that
\mu
and
\tilde{Z}
are properties of the CSM, which are conceptually distinct from the number of atoms used in SSPT simulations.
For example, MetaD calculations using up to
{\sim}10^{5}
atoms often yield CSMs with
\mu
and
\tilde{Z}
less than
{\sim}10^{1}
; see Table
2
.
Practically, we focus on solely those CSMs with finite multiplicity.

II.4
Congruence relations

Applying rigid transformations to
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
yields “congruent” yet set-theoretically different CSMs, as illustrated in Fig.
5
.
Such CSMs are equivalent as inputs to NEB-like methods, so we only need to enumerate all “noncongruent” CSMs.
We denote by
S\mathcal{A}+\mathbf{t}=\{S\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{t}\,|\,\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}\}
the crystal structure transformed from
\mathcal{A}
via an affine transformation
(S,\mathbf{t})
.
In the same sense as in Eq. (
10
), we have
(S,\mathbf{t})\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},S\mathcal{A}+\mathbf{t})
.

Definition 10 (Congruent CSM).

We say that a CSM
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is congruent to
\mathcal{J}\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
if there exist proper rigid transformations
(R_{A},\mathbf{t}_{A})
and
(R_{B},\mathbf{t}_{B})
such that:

(27)

i.e.,
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}=(R_{B},\mathbf{t}_{B})\mathcal{J}(R_{A},\mathbf{t}_{A})^{-1}
.

In general, two congruent CSMs have different domains and images, i.e., we have
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\notin\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
in Eq. (
27
), unless
(R_{A},\mathbf{t}_{A})
maps
\mathcal{A}
onto itself and
(R_{B},\mathbf{t}_{B})
does the same.
In this section, we specifically focus on the case where the proper rigid transformations are symmetry operations of the crystal structures, which define a congruence relation on
\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
.
We denote by
G_{A}
the group formed by all proper rigid transformations
(R,\mathbf{t})
such that

(28)

and define the point group

G_{A}^{\prime}=\left\{R\,\middle|\,(R,\mathbf{t})\in G_{A}\right\}.

(29)

It should be noted that
G_{A}^{\prime}
is
not
a subgroup of
G_{A}
unless the latter is symmorphic
Dresselhaus et al. 2007
, i.e.,
G_{A}=G_{A}^{\prime}\ltimes L_{A}
.
We will see that
G_{A}^{\prime}
and
G_{B}^{\prime}
induce an equivalence relation on
\operatorname{SLM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
, which is inherited from Definition
10
.

Lemma 1.

If
\mathcal{J}\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is a shuffle, then for any
\mathbf{t}_{A},\mathbf{t}_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the composite mapping
(+\mathbf{t}_{B})\circ\mathcal{J}\circ(-\mathbf{t}_{A})
is also a shuffle from
\mathcal{A}+\mathbf{t}_{A}
to
\mathcal{B}+\mathbf{t}_{B}
, whose shuffle lattice is the same as
\mathcal{J}
.

Theorem 3.

For any
(R_{A},\mathbf{t}_{A})\in G_{A}
,
(R_{B},\mathbf{t}_{B})\in G_{B}
, and
\mathcal{J}\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
, the CSM
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
defined by Eq. (
27
) is also in
\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
.
If
\mathcal{J}
has an SLM
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
, then
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
has the SLM
(R_{A}\tilde{L}_{A},R_{B}\tilde{L}_{B},R_{B}SR_{A}^{-1})
.

Proof.

When
(R_{A},\mathbf{t}_{A})\in G_{A}
and
(R_{B},\mathbf{t}_{B})\in G_{B}
, the bottom line of Eq.
27
becomes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
.
To show that
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
has the SLM
(R_{A}\tilde{L}_{A},R_{B}\tilde{L}_{B},R_{B}SR_{A}^{-1})
, we only need to show that the deformed CSM

\displaystyle[(R_{B},\mathbf{t}_{B})\mathcal{J}(R_{A},\mathbf{t}_{A})^{-1}](R_{B}SR_{A}^{-1})^{-1}

(30)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(R_{B},\mathbf{t}_{B})\mathcal{J}R_{A}^{-1}\circ(-\mathbf{t}_{A})\circ R_{A}S^{-1}R_{B}^{-1}

(31)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle(R_{B},\mathbf{t}_{B})\mathcal{J}S^{-1}R_{B}^{-1}\circ(-R_{B}SR_{A}^{-1}\mathbf{t}_{A})

(32)

is a shuffle with shuffle lattice
R_{B}\tilde{L}_{B}
.
By Definition
6
, a vector
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is in
\tilde{L}_{B}
if and only if we have:

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{94.86247pt}{-1.88889pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(34)

Taking the outer loop of the above diagram and using
(R_{B},\mathbf{t}_{B})\in G_{B}
, we can see that
\mathbf{t}\in\tilde{L}_{B}
if and only if:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{83.6056pt}{-1.13887pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

(35)

which implies that
(R_{B},\mathbf{t}_{B})\mathcal{J}S^{-1}(R_{B},\mathbf{t}_{B})^{-1}
is a shuffle with shuffle lattice
R_{B}\tilde{L}_{B}
.
Eq. (
32
) differs from this shuffle by only a translation, and is therefore also a shuffle with shuffle lattice
R_{B}\tilde{L}_{B}
according to Lemma
1
.
∎

Definition 11 (Congruent SLM).

We say that an SLM is congruent to
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)\in\operatorname{SLM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
if it can be written as
(R_{A}\tilde{L}_{A},R_{B}\tilde{L}_{B},R_{B}SR_{A}^{-1})
for some
R_{A}\in G_{A}^{\prime},R_{B}\in G_{B}^{\prime}
.

Before describing how to exhaustively enumerate CSMs, we need to clarify what kind of CSM set is
complete
.
We expect that they can, via NEB-like methods which allow rigid transformations, yield all SSPT mechanisms we are concerned with.
Hence, there is no need to enumerate two or more congruent CSMs, and we say that a subset of
X
comprising CSMs or SLMs is complete as long as it contains an element of each congruence class in
X
.

Theorem 4.

If
X
is a complete subset of
\operatorname{SLM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
, then
\bigcup_{x\in X}Y_{x}
is a complete subset of
\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
as long as
Y_{x}
is a complete subset of those CSMs with SLM
x
.

Proof.

As long as
\mathcal{J}\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
has an SLM, denoted by
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
, the completeness of
X
ensures that there exist
R_{A}\in G_{A}^{\prime}
and
R_{B}\in G_{B}^{\prime}
such that

(R_{A}\tilde{L}_{A},R_{B}\tilde{L}_{B},R_{B}SR_{A}^{-1})\in X.

(36)

Denote by
x
the left-hand side of Eq. (
36
).
Take
\mathbf{t}_{A},\mathbf{t}_{B}
such that
(R_{A},\mathbf{t}_{A})\in G_{A}
and
(R_{B},\mathbf{t}_{B})\in G_{B}
.
Since
\mathcal{J}
is congruent to
(R_{B},\mathbf{t}_{B})\mathcal{J}(R_{A},\mathbf{t}_{A})^{-1}
whose SLM is
x
(Theorem
3
), the transitivity of congruence means that
\mathcal{J}
is also congruent to some CSM in
Y_{x}
.
∎

Note that
\bigcup_{x\in X}Y_{x}
is also a complete subset of

\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}R_{1}\in\operatorname{SO}(3),\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\\
R_{2}\in\operatorname{SO}(3),\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\end{subarray}}\operatorname{CSM}(R_{1}\mathcal{A}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{1},R_{2}\mathcal{B}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{2}),

(37)

which incorporates all possible initial and final structures of an SSPT.
Hence, the CSMs in
\bigcup_{x\in X}Y_{x}
are sufficient as inputs to NEB-like methods.
We will demonstrate how to compute
X
and
Y_{x}
in Section
IV
.

III
Representation and pruning

Before we describe the
crystmatch
method for exhaustively enumerating all noncongruent CSMs, it is necessary to represent CSMs as
matrices
for computer processing.
We also need to introduce some physical constraints to make
\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
finite.
In this section, we will show how to represent SLM and shuffle separately, and discuss the general form of pruning criteria.
Afterwards, the finiteness of candidate CSMs will be proved.
Symbols frequently used in this section are summarized in Tables
3
.

III.1
Representing crystal-structure matches

As elaborated in Section
II
, a CSM with finite multiplicity can always be decomposed into an SLM and a shuffle, both of which are cell-independent.
However, just as an element of a vector space acquires a coordinate representation only after a basis is chosen, to represent CSMs as matrices, we also need to select a pair of “primitive cells” for
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
.

III.1.1
Representing sublattice matches

Definition 12 (Base Matrix).

Let
C\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
be a nonsingular matrix.
We say that
C(\mathbb{Z}^{3})=\left\{C\mathbf{k}\,\middle|\,\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\right\}
is the lattice generated by
C
, and
C
is the base matrix of
C(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
.

Lemma 2.

Any lattice has a base matrix.

Lemma 3.

Let
C,\tilde{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
be nonsingular matrices.

\tilde{C}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
is a sublattice of
C(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
if and only if there exists an
M\in\mathbb{Z}^{3\times 3}
such that
\tilde{C}=CM
.
In such case, the index of subgroup
\tilde{C}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
in
C(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
is
\lvert\det M\rvert
.

Lemma 4.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a crystal structure and
\tilde{L}_{A}
its sublattice.
Denote by
k
the index of subgroup
\tilde{L}_{A}
in
L_{A}
, which is finite by Lemmas
2
and
3
.
We have
\lvert\mathcal{A}/\tilde{L}_{A}\rvert=kZ_{A}
as long as
Z_{A}=\lvert\mathcal{A}/L_{A}\rvert
is finite.

Take
C_{A},C_{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
that generate
L_{A}
and
L_{B}
, respectively.
For each SLM
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
, there exists an
M_{A}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3\times 3}
such that
C_{A}M_{A}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})=\tilde{L}_{A}
.
From Eq. (
18
), we can see that
SC_{A}M_{A}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})=\tilde{L}_{B}
, so
SC_{A}M_{A}
is a base matrix of
\tilde{L}_{B}
which equals
C_{B}M_{B}
for some
M_{B}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3\times 3}
.
This provides a way to represent an SLM
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
by a pair of integer matrices
(M_{A},M_{B})
satisfying

\displaystyle C_{A}M_{A}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})

\displaystyle=\tilde{L}_{A},

(38)

\displaystyle C_{B}M_{B}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})

\displaystyle=\tilde{L}_{B},

(39)

\displaystyle SC_{A}M_{A}

\displaystyle=C_{B}M_{B},

(40)

\displaystyle Z_{A}\lvert\det M_{A}\rvert

\displaystyle=Z_{B}\lvert\det M_{B}\rvert,

(41)

where Eq. (
41
) is derived from Eq. (
17
) using Lemmas
3
and
4
.
We can also see that any nonsingular
3\times 3
integer matrix pair
(M_{A},M_{B})
satisfying Eq. (
41
) determines an SLM according to Eqs. (
38
–
40
).
This representation of SLM is intuitive and extensively used
Sheppard et al. 2012
;
Chen et al. 2016
;
Li and Liu 2022
;
Huang et al. 2024
, but is not suitable for SLM enumeration since many different
(M_{A},M_{B})
can yield the same SLM
Li and Liu 2022
;
Wang et al. 2024
.
To eliminate such representation redundancy, one may define a
canonical base matrix
for each lattice.
The concepts of unimodular matrices and Hermite normal form will be useful, just as they play a crucial role in the Hart-Forcade theory
Hart and Forcade 2008
.

Definition 13 (Unimodular Matrix).

Let
Q\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times n}
be a nonsingular integer matrix. We say that
Q
is unimodular if any of the following equivalent propositions holds:

\displaystyle Q(\mathbb{Z}^{n})=\mathbb{Z}^{n},

(42)

\displaystyle Q^{-1}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times n},

(43)

\displaystyle\det Q\in\{+1,-1\}.

(44)

We denote the matrix group formed by all unimodular matrices as
\operatorname{GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
.

Table 3:
Frequently used symbols in Section
III.1
.

Symbol

Meaning

\mathbb{F}^{n\times m}

All
n\times m
matrices over the field
\mathbb{F}

C(\mathbb{Z}^{3})

Lattice generated by nonsingular
C\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}

C_{A}

Base matrix of
L_{A}
(primitive-cell vectors)

\operatorname{GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})

All
n\times n
unimodular matrices

\operatorname{hnf}(M)

HNF of the integer matrix
M

\operatorname{HNF}(k)

All
3\times 3
HNFs with determinant
k

(H_{A},H_{B},Q)

IMT representation of an SLM

\operatorname{IMT}(\mu)

All IMTs with multiplicity
\mu

p

Permutation on the set
\{1,\cdots,\tilde{Z}\}

(p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}})

PCT representation of a shuffle

w(S)

Estimated strain energy density of
S

\sigma_{i}(S),s_{i}

i
-th largest singular value of
S

\tilde{\mathcal{J}}

Standard shuffle of
\mathcal{J}
(generally
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}\neq\mathcal{J}S^{-1}
)

\tilde{\mathcal{A}},\tilde{\mathcal{B}}

Half-distorted structures with sublattice
\tilde{L}

\tilde{L}

Shuffle lattice of
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}

\theta_{i}

Normalized weight of the
i
-th atom

\ell

Type of norm used to define shuffle distance

\hat{d}(\mathcal{J})

Shuffle distance of
\mathcal{J}

d(\mathcal{J})

Minimum
\hat{d}
among all CSMs congruent to
\mathcal{J}

Lemma 5.

Two nonsingular matrices
C,C^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
generate the same lattice if and only if
C^{-1}C^{\prime}\in\operatorname{GL}(3,\mathbb{Z})
.

Definition 14 (HNF).

Let
H\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times n}
be a nonsingular matrix.
We say that
H
is in Hermite normal form (HNF) if (1)
H
is lower triangular and (2) each off-diagonal element of
H
is nonnegative and
strictly
less than the diagonal element in its row.
In other words, the elements of
H
satisfy

\begin{cases}H_{ii}>H_{ij}\geq 0,&\text{if }j<i\\
H_{ij}=0,&\text{if }j>i\end{cases}

(45)

Lemma 6.

Let
M\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times n}
be a nonsingular matrix.
There exists a unique
H\in\mathbb{Z}^{n\times n}
in HNF and a unique
Q\in\operatorname{GL}(n,\mathbb{Z})
such that

\exists Q\in\operatorname{GL}(n,\mathbb{Z}),\quad M=HQ.

(46)

We say that
H
is the HNF of
M
.

Let
\operatorname{hnf}(M)
denote the HNF of
M
, and
\operatorname{HNF}(k)
denote all
3\times 3
HNFs with determinant
k
.
Lemma
5
implies that
(M_{A},M_{B})
and
(M_{A}^{\prime},M_{B}^{\prime})
represent the same SLM if and only if
(M_{A}^{\prime},M_{B}^{\prime})=(M_{A}Q^{\prime},M_{B}Q^{\prime})
for some
Q^{\prime}\in\operatorname{GL}(3,\mathbb{Z})
.
To make the representation of SLM unique, one may use the mapping
(M_{A},M_{B})\mapsto(H_{A},H_{B},Q)
, where

\displaystyle H_{A}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{hnf}(M_{A}),

(47)

\displaystyle H_{B}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{hnf}(M_{B}),

(48)

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=(H_{B}^{-1}M_{B})(H_{A}^{-1}M_{A})^{-1}.

(49)

It maps the entire
\{(M_{A}Q^{\prime},M_{B}Q^{\prime})\,|\,Q^{\prime}\in\operatorname{GL}(3,\mathbb{Z})\}
to a
single
integer-matrix triplet (IMT)
Wang et al. 2024
.

Definition 15 (IMT).

Let
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
be crystal structures and
\mu
a positive integer.
We say that

\operatorname{IMT}(\mu)=\operatorname{HNF}\!\left(\frac{\mu\operatorname{lcm}(Z_{A},Z_{B})}{Z_{A}}\right)\\
\times\operatorname{HNF}\!\left(\frac{\mu\operatorname{lcm}(Z_{A},Z_{B})}{Z_{B}}\right)\times\operatorname{GL}(3,\mathbb{Z})

(50)

is the set of all IMTs with multiplicity
\mu
.

Theorem 5.

The mapping
(H_{A},H_{B},Q)\mapsto(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
with

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{A}

\displaystyle=C_{A}H_{A}(\mathbb{Z}^{3}),

(51)

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{B}

\displaystyle=C_{B}H_{B}(\mathbb{Z}^{3}),

(52)

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=(C_{B}H_{B}Q)(C_{A}H_{A})^{-1}

(53)

is a bijection from
\operatorname{IMT}(\mu)
to
\operatorname{SLM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};\mu)
.

Proof.

(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
is an SLM since
S\tilde{L}_{A}=\tilde{L}_{B}
and

\displaystyle\lvert\mathcal{A}/\tilde{L}_{A}\rvert

\displaystyle=Z_{A}\det H_{A}

(54)

\displaystyle=\mu\operatorname{lcm}(Z_{A},Z_{B})

(55)

\displaystyle=Z_{B}\det H_{B}

(56)

\displaystyle=\lvert\mathcal{B}/\tilde{L}_{B}\rvert,

(57)

where Lemmas
3
and
4
are used to derive Eqs. (
54
) and (
57
).
Definition
15
ensures Eqs. (
55
) and (
56
), so that the multiplicity of
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
is
\mu
.
Conversely, for each
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)\in\operatorname{SLM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};\mu)
,
\tilde{L}_{A}
has a unique base matrix
C_{A}H_{A}
, where
H_{A}
is in HNF (Lemmas
5
and
6
) with determinant
\tilde{Z}/Z_{A}=\mu
(Lemmas
3
and
4
), and so does
\tilde{L}_{B}
.
Since
H_{A}
and
H_{B}
are determined,
S
further determines
Q
by Eq. (
53
).
Therefore, every SLM is associated with a unique IMT.
∎

Theorem
5
allows us to focus on the enumeration of
\operatorname{IMT}(\mu)
from now on, which is far more intuitive than the enumeration of SLMs.

III.1.2
Representing shuffles

Definition 16 (Motif).

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a crystal structure and
\tilde{L}_{A}
a sublattice of
\mathcal{A}
.
We say that
\{\mathbf{a}_{1},\mathbf{a}_{2}\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{n}\}\subset\mathcal{A}
is an
\tilde{L}_{A}
-motif of
\mathcal{A}
if
\mathbf{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{n}
are
\tilde{L}_{A}
-inequivalent and
n=\lvert\mathcal{A}/\tilde{L}_{A}\rvert
.

Theorem 6.

Let
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
be an SLM with period
\tilde{Z}
,
\{\mathbf{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{\tilde{Z}}\}
an
\tilde{L}_{A}
-motif of
\mathcal{A}
, and
\{\mathbf{b}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{b}_{\tilde{Z}}\}
an
\tilde{L}_{B}
-motif of
\mathcal{B}
.
If
\mathcal{J}\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
has the SLM
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
, there exist a unique permutation
p\in\operatorname{Sym}(\tilde{Z})
and unique
\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}\in\tilde{L}_{B}
such that

\forall i\in\{1,\cdots,\tilde{Z}\},\quad\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a}_{i})=\mathbf{b}_{p(i)}+\mathbf{t}_{i}.

(58)

Furthermore, if both
\mathcal{J}_{1}
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
have
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
, then
\mathcal{J}_{1}=\mathcal{J}_{2}
if and only if they have the same
(p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}})
.

Proof.

In the proof of Theorem
2
, we have already shown that
\mathbf{a}+\tilde{L}_{A}\mapsto\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a})+\tilde{L}_{B}
is a bijection from
\mathcal{A}/\tilde{L}_{A}
to
\mathcal{B}/\tilde{L}_{B}
.
Therefore, the images of different
\mathbf{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{\tilde{Z}}
under
\mathcal{J}
are in different
\mathbf{b}_{1}+\tilde{L}_{B},\cdots,\mathbf{b}_{\tilde{Z}}+\tilde{L}_{B}
.
This determines
p
which is a permutation on
\{1,\cdots,\tilde{Z}\}
, and
\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}
is also uniquely determined by Eq. (
58
).
Furthermore, since each element in
\mathcal{A}
can be uniquely decomposed as
\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{a}_{i}+\mathbf{t}
for some
i\in\{1,\cdots,\tilde{Z}\}
and
\mathbf{t}\in\tilde{L}_{A}
, we have

\forall\mathbf{a}_{i}+\mathbf{t}\in\mathcal{A},\quad\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a}_{i}+\mathbf{t})=\mathbf{b}_{p(i)}+\mathbf{t}_{i}+S\mathbf{t},

(59)

which is derived from Eq. (
58
) and the following commutative diagram:
