{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.82133pt}{-43.16698pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(60)

From Eq. (
59
) we can see that
(p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}})
completely determines a CSM with the SLM
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
.
∎

Given the SLM, Theorem
6
uniquely represents each CSM as a permutation
p
and
\tilde{Z}
elements of
\tilde{L}_{B}
.
The underlying reason is that each
(p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}})
actually represents a shuffle, which we refer to as the “permutation with class-wise translations” (PCT) representation, as illustrated in Fig.
6
.
We denote by
\mathcal{J}_{p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}}
the CSM determined by Eq. (
59
).

Figure 6:
Two PCTs representing
\mathcal{J}_{1},\mathcal{J}_{2}\colon S\mathcal{A}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{B}
with the same shuffle lattice (gray grids), respectively.
Both
\{S\mathbf{a}_{i}\}
, the motif of
S\mathcal{A}
(cyan) and
\{\mathbf{b}_{i}\}
, the motif of
\mathcal{B}
(blue) are labeled by
1-4
.
Every pink atom
\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a}_{i})
is both the image of
S\mathbf{a}_{i}
under
\mathcal{J}S^{-1}
and an element of
\mathcal{B}
, which can be written as
\mathbf{b}_{p(i)}+\mathbf{t}_{i}
.
Therefore, the PCT representation of a shuffle can be viewed as the unique map
\mathbf{b}_{i}\mapsto\mathbf{b}_{p(i)}+\mathbf{t}_{i}
that makes the triangle in the figure commute, where the horizontal gray arrow represents
S\mathbf{a}_{i}\mapsto\mathbf{b}_{i}
(the canonical bijection between motifs).
The pink text shows the specific PCTs representing
\mathcal{J}_{1}
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
, where
p=(\begin{smallmatrix}1&2&3&4\\
1&3&2&4\end{smallmatrix})
.

Definition 17 (PCT).

Given a mapping

(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)\mapsto(\mathbf{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{\tilde{Z}},\mathbf{b}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{b}_{\tilde{Z}}),

(61)

that determines an
\tilde{L}_{A}
-motif of
\mathcal{A}
and an
\tilde{L}_{B}
-motif of
\mathcal{B}
for each SLM in
\operatorname{SLM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
.
We say that

\operatorname{PCT}(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)=\Big\{(p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}})\in\operatorname{Sym}(\tilde{Z})\times\tilde{L}_{B}^{\tilde{Z}}\,\Big|\\
\forall i,\,\chi_{A}(\mathbf{a}_{i})=\chi_{B}(\mathbf{b}_{p(i)})\Big\}

(62)

is the set of all PCTs with SLM
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
, which depends only on the SLM and the mapping.

Note that
p
can be stored as a permutation matrix, and
\tilde{L}_{B}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
.
In this sense, all the ingredients of a CSM can be represented as integer matrices.
Given
C_{A}
,
C_{B}
, and a deterministic way to choose the
\tilde{L}_{A}
- and
\tilde{L}_{B}
-motifs for each SLM, one can reproduce any CSM from its associated IMT and PCT.

III.2
Pruning criteria

As a one-to-one correspondence between infinite sets, the CSM has infinitely many possibilities.
Fortunately, we are only concerned with the CSMs of those MEPs with low energy barriers.
Hence, many CSMs can be excluded, leaving only a finite number of candidates.

III.2.1
Strain energy density

From an energetic perspective, the constraint on strain is necessary, since excessive deformation would lead to a large strain energy.
We are only concerned with those CSMs whose deformation gradients satisfy

w(S)\leq w_{\text{max}},

(63)

where
w(S)
denotes the strain energy density associated with the deformation gradient
S
, and
w_{\text{max}}
is a truncation threshold.
When searching for the CSM of the lowest-barrier MEP, one may let
w_{\text{max}}
be the minimum
\Delta^{\ddagger}
-density among known SSPTs.

Lemma 7 (SVD).

If
S\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
has a positive determinant, there exist
U,V\in\operatorname{SO}(3)
and unique
s_{1}\geq s_{2}\geq s_{3}>0
such that
S=U\Sigma V^{\text{T}}
, where
\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})
.

Lemma
7
is known as the singular value decomposition (SVD) and
s_{i}
is referred to as the
i
-th singular value of
S

Axler 2015
, usually denoted by
\sigma_{i}(S)
.
A direct corollary is the polar decomposition (PD), which states that a deformation gradient can always be
uniquely
factorized into a positive-definite transformation and a rotation, as

S=(UV^{\text{T}})\left(V\Sigma V^{\text{T}}\right)=\left(S\sqrt{S^{\text{T}}S}^{\,-1}\right)\sqrt{S^{\text{T}}S}.

(64)

Note that the rotation
UV^{\text{T}}
does not contribute to the strain energy, and the positive-definite
V\Sigma V^{\text{T}}
simply scales the space by
s_{i}
along
\mathbf{v}_{i}
for
i=1,2,3
, where
\mathbf{v}_{i}
denotes the
i
-th column of
V
.
We estimate the strain energy according to the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}V^{\text{T}}\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\Sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}U^{\text{T}}\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\Sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}U^{\text{T}}\mathcal{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{J}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces UV^{\text{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqrt{V\Sigma V^{\text{T}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqrt{U\Sigma U^{\text{T}}}}

(65)

where
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}
is defined as the unique shuffle that makes the diagram commute, which we refer to as the
standard shuffle
of
\mathcal{J}
.
Note that the two vertical arrows are pure deformations, while all horizontal arrows in the second row involve no strain.
Based on this,
w
is estimated as the sum of the strain energies associated with the deformation of
\mathcal{A}
by
\sqrt{V\Sigma V^{\text{T}}}
and the deformation of
\mathcal{B}
by
\sqrt{U\Sigma U^{\text{T}}}^{\,-1}
, namely,

\displaystyle w(S)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{3}\left\{Y_{ijkl}^{A}\left[V(\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}-I)V^{\text{T}}\right]_{ij}\left[V(\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}-I)V^{\text{T}}\right]_{kl}+Y_{ijkl}^{B}\left[U(\Sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}-I)U^{\text{T}}\right]_{ij}\left[U(\Sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}-I)U^{\text{T}}\right]_{kl}\right\}

(66)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a,b=1}^{3}\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{3}\left[\left(\sqrt{s_{a}}-1\right)\left(\sqrt{s_{b}}-1\right)Y_{ijkl}^{A}V_{ia}V_{ja}V_{kb}V_{lb}+\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{s_{a}}}-1\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{s_{b}}}-1\right)Y_{ijkl}^{B}U_{ia}U_{ja}U_{kb}U_{lb}\right],

(67)

where
Y_{ijkl}^{A}
and
Y_{ijkl}^{B}
denote the elastic (stiffness) tensors of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, respectively.

Substituting Eq. (
67
) into Eq. (
63
) yields the inequality that
S
must satisfy, which varies depending on the material’s mechanical properties.
It is worth noting that
w
is a
congruence-class function
, i.e.,

\forall R_{A}\in G_{A}^{\prime},\quad\forall R_{B}\in G_{B}^{\prime},\quad w(R_{B}SR_{A}^{-1})=w(S).

(68)

This can be seen from Lemma
7
, where
R_{A}
and
R_{B}
only contributes to
V
and
U
, respectively.
As symmetry operations of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, the elastic tensors
Y_{ijkl}^{A}
and
Y_{ijkl}^{B}
are invariant under
R_{A}
and
R_{B}
, respectively.
Hence, SLMs are pruned by congruence classes under Eq. (
63
).

III.2.2
Shuffle distance

For each SLM, there are still infinitely many possible CSMs—even a single atom
\mathbf{a}\in\mathcal{A}
has infinitely many choices of its counterpart
\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a})
.
An intuitive constraint is that
\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a})
should not be too far from
\mathbf{a}
.
This is not an energetic consideration, but rather an empirical one, as discussed in Ref.
25
, where the average distance traveled by the atoms is shown to be a key indicator.
However, the average distance between
\mathcal{J}(\mathbf{a})
and
\mathbf{a}
goes to infinity unless
S=I
, i.e.,
\mathcal{J}
is a shuffle.
Therefore, the appropriate way to define the “average distance” is to consider a shuffle associated with
\mathcal{J}
.
Here, we use the standard shuffle
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}
defined in Eq. (
65
) instead of
\mathcal{J}S^{-1}
to ensure the metric symmetry between the initial and final structures.
As shorthands, denote by
\tilde{L}
the shuffle lattice of
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}
and let
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}=V\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}V^{\text{T}}\mathcal{A}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}=V\Sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}U^{\text{T}}\mathcal{B}
, which are all uniquely determined by the SLM.

Definition 18 (Shuffle Distance).

Let
\mathcal{J}\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
be a CSM with period
\tilde{Z}
.
We say that the shuffle distance of
\mathcal{J}
is

\hat{d}(\mathcal{J})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{Z}}\theta_{i}\left|\tilde{\mathcal{J}}(\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i})-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i}\right|^{\ell}\right)^{1/\ell},

(69)

where
\ell\geq 1
specifies the norm,
\{\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{\tilde{Z}}\}
is an
\tilde{L}
-motif of
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
, and
\theta_{i}>0
is a normalized weight that only depends on the atomic species of
\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i}
(e.g., a constant, or atomic mass).
One may adopt
\ell=2
for the root-mean-square distance (RMSD), or
\ell=1
for the arithmetic mean distance.

Eq.
9
ensures the value of Eq. (
69
) to be independent of the choice of the motif so that
\hat{d}
is well-defined.
However, congruent CSMs may not necessarily have the same
\hat{d}
, which is undesirable since they are equivalent as inputs to NEB-like methods.
To address this issue, we define

d(\mathcal{J})=\min_{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{Z}}\theta_{i}\lvert\tilde{\mathcal{J}}(\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i})+\boldsymbol{\tau}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i}\rvert^{\ell}\right)^{1/\ell},

(70)

which equals the minimum
\hat{d}
among all CSMs of the form
(+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{B})\circ\mathcal{J}\circ(-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{A})\in\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{A},\mathcal{B}+\boldsymbol{\tau}_{B})
.
Similar to the proof of Theorem
3
, one can show that any
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
congruent to
\mathcal{J}
has a standard shuffle that differs from
(R,\mathbf{t})\tilde{\mathcal{J}}(R,\mathbf{t})^{-1}
by just a translation, implying that

d(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})=d[(R,\mathbf{t})\tilde{\mathcal{J}}(R,\mathbf{t})^{-1}]=d(\tilde{\mathcal{J}})=d(\mathcal{J}),

(71)

where we have used the fact that
(R,\mathbf{t})
is an isometry.
Eq. (
71
) means that congruent CSMs have the same
d
, so that as long as
d(\mathcal{J})
exceeds the threshold
d_{\text{max}}
, we can exclude all CSMs congruent to
\mathcal{J}
.

III.2.3
Finiteness of candidate crystal-structure matches

As shown in Fig.
2
, the enumeration includes a CSM
\mathcal{J}
with multiplicity
\mu
and deformation gradient
S
if

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\text{max}},

(72)

\displaystyle w(S)

\displaystyle\leq w_{\text{max}},

(73)

\displaystyle d(\mathcal{J})

\displaystyle\leq d_{\text{max}}.

(74)

They have all been detailed earlier, except for Eq. (
72
), which can be regarded as an empirical constraint inferred from Table
2
—despite MD and MetaD simulations using far more than
10^{3}
atoms, the intricacy of the CSMs they produce remain
\tilde{Z}\sim 10^{1}
.
We emphasize that
w_{\text{max}}
can depend on
\mu
, and
d_{\text{max}}
can depend on the SLM of
\mathcal{J}
.
For example, one might want to slacken the constraints on
w_{\text{max}}
and
d_{\text{max}}
when
\mu
is small.
One may also set
d_{\text{max}}
for each SLM to
110\%
of the lowest
d
value among all CSMs with that SLM.
In a word, different subtrees in Fig.
2
can be pruned with different thresholds.
We denote by
\operatorname{CSM}^{\star}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
the set of all CSMs in
\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
that satisfy Eqs. (
72
–
74
), and their SLMs by
\operatorname{SLM}^{\star}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
.

Theorem 7.

\operatorname{SLM}^{\star}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};\mu)
is finite regardless of the values of
\mu
and
w_{\text{max}}
.

Proof.

There exists a
\sigma_{\text{max}}
such that
\sigma_{1}(S)<\sigma_{\text{max}}
implies Eq. (
73
); otherwise, infinite strain would leads to infinite strain energy.
We only need to prove that only a finite number of
(H_{A},H_{B},Q)\in\operatorname{IMT}(\mu)
satisfy
\sigma_{1}(S)<\sigma_{\text{max}}
.
Since
\operatorname{HNF}(k)
is finite, the number of possible values of
(H_{A},H_{B})
is already finite.
It is sufficient to show that given
(H_{A},H_{B})
, the number of possible values of
Q
is finite.
This is due to the fact that the absolute value of any matrix element of
Q
does not exceed

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}(Q)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{1}(H_{B}^{-1}C_{B}^{-1}SC_{A}H_{A})

(75)

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{1}(H_{B}^{-1})\sigma_{1}(C_{B}^{-1})\sigma_{1}(C_{A})\sigma_{1}(H_{A})\,\sigma_{\text{max}},

(76)

where Eq. (
75
) is derived from Eq. (
53
)
∎

Theorem 8.

For any given SLM and
d_{\text{max}}
, the number of noncongruent CSMs that have this SLM and satisfy Eq. (
74
) is finite.

Proof.

We assume by contradiction that there are infinitely many noncongruent CSMs in
\operatorname{CSM}^{\star}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
that have
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
.
Among them, each
\mathcal{J}_{p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\mathbf{t}_{2},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}}
is congruent to a
\mathcal{J}_{p,\mathbf{0},\mathbf{t}_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}^{\prime}}
, where
\mathbf{t}_{i}^{\prime}=\mathbf{t}_{i}-\mathbf{t}_{1}
.
Since
p
can take only a finite number of values, there are infinitely many PCTs having the same
p
and different
(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{t}_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}^{\prime})
that satisfy

d(\mathcal{J}_{p,\mathbf{0},\mathbf{t}_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}^{\prime}})\leq d_{\text{max}}.

(77)

These
(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{t}_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}^{\prime})
form an infinite subset of
\tilde{L}_{B}^{\tilde{Z}}
, so there exist
j
such that
\lvert\mathbf{t}_{j}^{\prime}\rvert
is unbounded; otherwise, the subset would be finite.
Let

y=\left|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(j)}+\tilde{\mathbf{t}}_{j}^{\prime}-\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(1)}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{j}+\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{1}\right|,

(78)

which is also unbounded. We have

\displaystyle[d(\mathcal{J}_{p,\mathbf{0},\mathbf{t}_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}^{\prime}})]^{\ell}

\displaystyle=\min_{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{Z}}\theta_{i}\left|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(i)}+\tilde{\mathbf{t}}_{i}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\tau}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i}\right|^{\ell}

(79)

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(\theta_{1}\left|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(1)}+\boldsymbol{\tau}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{1}\right|^{\ell}+\theta_{j}\left|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(j)}+\tilde{\mathbf{t}}_{j}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{\tau}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{j}\right|^{\ell}\right)

(80)

\displaystyle=\min_{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(\theta_{1}\left|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(1)}+\boldsymbol{\tau}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{1}\right|^{\ell}+\theta_{j}\left|\left(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(1)}+\boldsymbol{\tau}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{1}\right)+\left(\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(j)}+\tilde{\mathbf{t}}_{j}^{\prime}-\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(1)}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{j}+\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{1}\right)\right|^{\ell}\right)

(81)

\displaystyle\geq\min_{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(\theta_{1}\left|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(1)}+\boldsymbol{\tau}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{1}\right|^{\ell}+\theta_{j}\bigg|\left|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(1)}+\boldsymbol{\tau}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{1}\right|-\left|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(j)}+\tilde{\mathbf{t}}_{j}^{\prime}-\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(1)}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{j}+\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{1}\right|\bigg|^{\ell}\right)

(82)

\displaystyle=\min_{x\geq 0}\left(\theta_{1}x^{\ell}+\theta_{j}\lvert x-y\rvert^{\ell}\right)=\begin{cases}y\min\{\theta_{1},\theta_{j}\},&\text{if}~\ell=1\\
y^{\ell}\left(\theta_{1}^{-\ell+1}+\theta_{j}^{-\ell+1}\right)^{-\ell+1},&\text{if}~\ell>1\end{cases}.

(83)

Since
y
is unbounded, Eq. (
83
) can be arbitrarily large, which contradicts Eq. (
77
).
∎

Theorem 9.

The number of noncongruent CSMs in
\operatorname{CSM}^{\star}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is finite regardless of
\mu_{\text{max}}
,
w_{\text{max}}
, and
d_{\text{max}}
.

Proof.

Replace the root node “all CSMs” in Fig.
2
with “all noncongruent CSMs”.
Theorems
7
and
8
ensure that the breadth of the pruned tree is finite at each level.
∎

IV
Algorithms

To enumerate all noncongruent CSMs in
\operatorname{CSM}^{\star}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
, Theorem
4
allows us to enumerate all noncongruent SLMs in
\operatorname{SLM}^{\star}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
first, and then all noncongruent CSMs within each SLM.
With Theorems
5
and
6
, we only need to generate a complete subset of

\operatorname{IMT}^{\star}(\mu)=\big\{(H_{A},H_{B},Q)\in\operatorname{IMT}(\mu)\,\big|\\
w(C_{B}H_{B}QH_{A}^{-1}C_{A}^{-1})\leq w_{\text{max}}\big\}

(84)

for
\mu=1,\cdots,\mu_{\text{max}}
, and a complete subset of

\operatorname{PCT}^{\star}(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)=\big\{(p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}})\\
\in\operatorname{PCT}(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)\,\big|\,d(\mathcal{J}_{p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}})\leq d_{\text{max}}\big\}

(85)

for each
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)\in\operatorname{SLM}^{\star}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B};\mu)
.

In this section, we will introduce Algorithm
1
for IMTs as well as Algorithms
2
and
3
for PCTs, and then apply them on some typical SSPTs.
These algorithms do not involve electronic structure or atomic force field calculations, i.e., they are purely geometric.
The pseudocodes are presented in an easy-to-understand form, but the actual implementation can be more efficient, e.g., all loops in Algorithm
1
can be parallelized.

IV.1
Enumerating sublattice matches

Let’s first consider how to produce the entire
\operatorname{IMT}^{\star}(\mu)
rather than its complete subset.
It is easy to enumerate all possible values of
H_{A}
and
H_{B}
, so the difficulty lies solely in computing all possible
Q
for a given HNF pair
(H_{A},H_{B})
, i.e., producing
\{Q\,|\,(H_{A},H_{B},Q)\in\operatorname{IMT}^{\star}(\mu)\}
, which we denote by
\{Q\}
for simplicity.
To this end, we first sample the 9D region

\Omega_{S_{0}}=\left\{S_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}\,\middle|\,w(S_{0})\leq w_{\text{max}}\right\},

(86)

producing a finite set consisting of
trial
matrices, denoted as
\{S_{0}\}\subset\Omega_{S_{0}}
.
Then, for each trial matrix
S_{0}
, we compute the closest IMTs by (1) substituting
S_{0}
into Eq. (
53
), (2) solve for
Q
for each pair
(H_{A},H_{B})
, and (3) round each matrix element of
Q
to the nearest integer.
In other words, we apply the linear mapping

\displaystyle\phi_{H_{A},H_{B}}\colon\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3},

(87)

\displaystyle S_{0}

\displaystyle\mapsto H_{B}^{-1}C_{B}^{-1}S_{0}C_{A}H_{A},

(88)

to the set
\{S_{0}\}
.
As long as
\{S_{0}\}
is sampled dense enough, its image under
\phi_{H_{A},H_{B}}
will intersect each
Q
’s “preimage of rounding”

\operatorname{round}^{-1}(Q)=\left\{Q+X\,\middle|\,X\in\left(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right)^{3\times 3}\right\},

(89)

a 9D hypercube centered at
Q
[Fig.
7
(b)].
In that case, we can extract
\{Q\}
from
\operatorname{round}[\phi_{H_{A},H_{B}}(\{S_{0}\})]
.

Figure 7:
A schematic diagram showing how Algorithm
1
works, where 9D regions are drawn as 2D shapes for intuitive understanding.
(a)
\Omega_{S_{0}}
(cyan) is a neighborhood of
\operatorname{SO}(3)
determined solely by
Y^{A}_{ijkl}
,
Y^{B}_{ijkl}
and
w_{\text{max}}
.
The trail matrices
\{S_{0}\}
(blue diamonds) are generated in
\Omega_{S_{0}}
, approximating the exact deformation gradients (red stars).
(b)
\phi_{H_{A},H_{B}}
is applied to
\{S_{0}\}
, and then each
\phi_{H_{A},H_{B}}(S_{0})
is rounded to the nearest integer matrix (gray dots).
Each element of
\{Q\}
(red stars in cyan region) is produced if and only if a
S_{0}
lies within its “preimage of rounding” (pink squares).
Currently, the algorithm should continue to generate a denser
\{S_{0}\}
to produce the topmost
Q
in the figure.

The only remaining problem is how to tell whether the set
\{S_{0}\}
is dense
enough
such that
\{Q\}
is covered by its image under
\phi_{H_{A},H_{B}}
.
Our approach is to generate
S_{0}
one by one until
i_{\text{th}}
consecutive iterations fail to produce any new IMT, as described in Algorithm
1
.
The value of
i_{\text{th}}
can be derived by hypothesis testing.
Whenever the algorithm starts or a new IMT is produced, we initiate a test of the two opposing hypotheses, namely

\displaystyle H_{0}\colon

\displaystyle\text{The current }\mathsf{myimts}\text{ is complete},

\displaystyle H_{1}\colon

\displaystyle\text{The current }\mathsf{myimts}\text{ is incomplete}.

Then, when we perform
i
iterations without producing any new IMT, the conditional probability of this event under
H_{0}
is
1
, whereas under
H_{1}
it is only

\Pr(i\,|\,H_{1})\leq(1-\eta)^{i},

(90)

where
\eta
denotes the minimum probability of a given IMT being generated in a single iteration.
If one would reject
H_{1}
when
\Pr(i\,|\,H_{1})<\epsilon
, it is sufficient to set

i_{\text{th}}=\frac{\ln\epsilon}{\ln(1-\eta)}.

(91)

Note that the probability
\epsilon
of incorrectly asserting completeness decreases exponentially with increasing
i_{\text{th}}
.
In this sense, we claim that Algorithm
1
is essentially an
exhaustive
search algorithm.

Input:

C_{A}
,
C_{B}
,
Z_{A}
,
Z_{B}
,
\mu
,
w_{\text{max}}

initialize
\textnormal{{myimts}}\leftarrow\{\}
;
i\leftarrow 0

while

i\leq i_{\text{th}}

do

generate a random
S_{0}\in\Omega_{S_{0}}

foreach

H_{A}\in\operatorname{HNF}\!\left(\frac{\mu\operatorname{lcm}(Z_{A},Z_{B})}{Z_{A}}\right)

do

foreach

H_{B}\in\operatorname{HNF}\!\left(\frac{\mu\operatorname{lcm}(Z_{A},Z_{B})}{Z_{B}}\right)

do

Q\leftarrow\operatorname{round}(H_{B}^{-1}C_{B}^{-1}S_{0}C_{A}H_{A})

S\leftarrow C_{B}H_{B}QH_{A}^{-1}C_{A}^{-1}

if

Q\in\operatorname{GL}(3,\mathbb{Z})

and

w(S)\leq w_{\text{max}}

then

x\leftarrow

Standardize(
H_{A},H_{B},Q
)

if

x\notin\textnormal{{myimts}}

then

add
x
to
myimts

i\leftarrow 0

else

i\leftarrow i+1

return

myimts

Algorithm 1

Listing all noncongruent IMTs

Recall that our goal is to list all noncongruent elements in
\operatorname{IMT}^{\star}(\mu)
.
We can treat each IMT as its congruence class—whenever an IMT is generated, if it is congruent to some element already in
\mathsf{myimts}
, it is considered not new and
i
increases.
However, a better way might be using a
Standardize()
function to convert each IMT into the representative of its congruence class (e.g., the lexicographically smallest one), as shown in the pseudocode.
Either way will replace
1-\eta
in Eq. (
91
) with the minimum probability that no IMT from a given congruence class is generated in a single iteration.
A conservative estimate of
i_{\text{th}}
is provided in Appendix
B
, while in practice,
i_{\text{th}}
can be dynamically adjusted to accelerate the algorithm.

IV.2
Enumerating shuffles

To list all noncongruent PCTs, Algorithm
2
involves three functions: (1)
OptimizePCT()
for finding the PCT with the minimum
d
, (2)
Fill()
for generating all noncongruent PCTs with a given
p
, and (3)
Split()
for excluding previously enumerated values of
p
.
All we need to do is to iteratively invoke them, as shown in Fig.
8
.
Next, we will introduce the specific algorithms for implementing these three functions separately.

Input:

\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
,
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
,
d_{\text{max}}

initialize
\textnormal{{mypcts}}\leftarrow\{\}
;
\textnormal{{myconstraints}}\leftarrow
empty queue

enqueue
\{\}
to
myconstraints

while

myconstraints
is not empty
do

dequeue
constraint
from
myconstraints

d,(p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}})\leftarrow

OptimizePCT(
constraint

)

if

d\leq d_{\text{max}}

then

foreach

(\mathbf{t}_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}^{\prime})\in

Fill(
p
)

do

add
(p,\mathbf{0},\mathbf{t}_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}^{\prime})
to
mypcts

foreach

\textnormal{{newcons}}\in

Split(
\textnormal{{constraint}},p
)

do

enqueue
newcons
to
myconstraints

return

mypcts

Algorithm 2

Listing all noncongruent PCTs

Figure 8:
A schematic diagram showing how Algorithm
2
works, where each point represents a PCT.
First,
OptimizePCT()
is invoked to obtain the PCT with the smallest
d
.
Then,
Fill()
generates all PCTs that share the same permutation with it, while
Split()
produces a set of constraints to disable this permutation in subsequent computations.
By passing these constraints as parameters to
OptimizePCT()
, other bottom PCTs (dots in blue circles) can be generated.

Input:

constraint
, and all inputs of Algorithm
2

\mathbf{a}_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{\tilde{Z}_{1}}^{(1)},\mathbf{a}_{1}^{(2)},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{\tilde{Z}_{2}}^{(2)},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{1}^{(n)},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{\tilde{Z}_{n}}^{(n)},\mathbf{b}_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,\mathbf{b}_{\tilde{Z}_{1}}^{(1)},\mathbf{b}_{1}^{(2)},\cdots,\mathbf{b}_{\tilde{Z}_{2}}^{(2)},\cdots,\mathbf{b}_{1}^{(n)},\cdots,\mathbf{b}_{\tilde{Z}_{n}}^{(n)}\leftarrow

Motifs(
\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S
)

def

ShuffleDistance(
\boldsymbol{\tau},\textnormal{{returnpct}}=\mathit{false}
)

for

\alpha=1

to

n

do

for

i=1

to

\tilde{Z}_{\alpha}

do

for

j=1

to

\tilde{Z}_{\alpha}

do

\mathbf{t}_{ij}^{(\alpha)}\leftarrow\operatorname{argmin}_{\mathbf{t}\in\tilde{L}_{B}}\left\lvert\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{j}^{(\alpha)}+\tilde{\mathbf{t}}+\boldsymbol{\tau}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right\rvert^{\ell}
;
D_{ij}^{(\alpha)}\leftarrow\left\lvert\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{j}^{(\alpha)}+\tilde{\mathbf{t}}_{ij}^{(\alpha)}+\boldsymbol{\tau}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right\rvert^{\ell}

p^{(\alpha)}\leftarrow

BestAssignment(
D^{(\alpha)},\textnormal{{constraint}}
)
;
for

i=1

to

\tilde{Z}_{\alpha}

do

\mathbf{t}_{i}^{(\alpha)}\leftarrow\mathbf{t}_{i,p^{(\alpha)}(i)}^{(\alpha)}

d\leftarrow\left(\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{Z}_{\alpha}}\theta_{i}^{(\alpha)}D_{i,p^{(\alpha)}(i)}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{1/\ell}

if

returnpct

then

return

d,\Big((p^{(1)}|p^{(2)}|\cdots|p^{(n)}),\mathbf{t}_{1}^{(1)},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}_{1}}^{(1)},\mathbf{t}_{1}^{(2)},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}_{2}}^{(2)},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{1}^{(n)},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}_{n}}^{(n)}\Big)

else

return

d

\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}\leftarrow\operatorname{argmin}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}

ShuffleDistance(
\boldsymbol{\tau}
)
;
return

ShuffleDistance(
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*},\textnormal{{returnpct}}=\mathit{true}
)

Algorithm 3

Optimizing PCT under a given constraint (producing the PCT with least shuffle distance)

We use
OptimizePCT(
constraint
)
to generate the PCT with the smallest
d
under the constraints specified by
constraint
.
In each iteration step, the overall translation
\boldsymbol{\tau}
is fixed, and the best translation
\mathbf{t}_{ij}\in\tilde{L}_{B}
for each atom pair
(\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{b}_{j})
is computed.
Then, the best permutation
p
under this
\boldsymbol{\tau}
is determined using
BestAssignment()
(e.g., the Jonker-Volgenant algorithm
Jonker and Volgenant 1987
;
Crouse 2016
), so that
\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}
and

\hat{d}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\tau})=\min_{p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}}\hat{d}[(+\boldsymbol{\tau})\circ\mathcal{J}_{p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}}]

(92)

are also obtained.
This process is iterated with different
\boldsymbol{\tau}
values until the global minimum of
\hat{d}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\tau})
is found, where strategies such as basin-hopping are employed
Wales and Doye 1997
.
The final iteration uses
\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*}=\operatorname{argmin}_{\boldsymbol{\tau}}\hat{d}_{0}(\boldsymbol{\tau})
, at which point the PCT given by
BestAssignment()
is

\displaystyle\quad\,\mathop{\operatorname{argmin}}_{p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}}\hat{d}[(+\boldsymbol{\tau}^{*})\circ\mathcal{J}_{p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}}]

(93)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\operatorname{argmin}}_{p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}}\min_{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\hat{d}[(+\boldsymbol{\tau})\circ\mathcal{J}_{p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}}]

(94)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\operatorname{argmin}}_{p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}}d(\mathcal{J}_{p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}}),

(95)

which is exactly what we need.

To formalize this idea and handle atomic species, the pseodocode is presented in Algorithm
3
.
The function
Motifs(
\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S
)
is a mapping like Eq. (
61
) but also sorts the atoms by atomic species, where
n
denotes the number of atomic species in the initial and final structures,
\tilde{Z}_{\alpha}
denotes the number of atoms with the
\alpha
-th species, and other variables related to the
\alpha
-th atomic species are denoted with superscripts as
\mathbf{a}_{i}^{(\alpha)}
.
The function
BestAssignment(
D,\mathsf{constraint}
)
returns a permutation
p
that minimizes
\sum_{i}D_{i,p(i)}
, where
D
is the cost matrix and
constraint
is a set of the form

\left\{(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),\cdots;\overline{(k,l)},\overline{(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})},\cdots\right\},

(96)

where
(i,j)
means enforcing
p(i)=j
, and
\overline{(k,l)}
means requiring
p(k)\neq l

Murty 1968
.
The correspondence of each atomic species is optimized separately and then combined into a single PCT, where
(p^{(1)}|p^{(2)}|\cdots|p^{(n)})\in\operatorname{Sym}(\tilde{Z})
represents performing
n
permutations separately within
n
atomic species.

We say that
(p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}})
is a bottom PCT if its
d
is the smallest among all PCTs with the same permutation.
Whenever we obtain a bottom PCT, we invoke
Fill(
p
)
to list all PCTs of the form
(p,\mathbf{0},\mathbf{t}_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}^{\prime})
that satisfy Eq.
77
.
Here, we have let
\mathbf{t}_{1}^{\prime}=\mathbf{0}
to make the number of PCTs finite without loss of completeness (see the proof of Lemma
8
).
Since
\mathbf{v}_{i}=\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(i)}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i}
is constant during each invocation of
Fill()
, one may simply use a flood fill algorithm to produce

\big\{(\mathbf{t}_{2}^{\prime},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}^{\prime})\in\tilde{L}_{B}^{\tilde{Z}-1}\big|\\
\min_{\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{Z}}\theta_{i}\lvert\mathbf{v}_{i}+\tilde{\mathbf{t}}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\tau}\rvert^{\ell}\leq d_{\text{max}}^{\ell}\big\}.

(97)

When
\ell=2
, Lemma
8
implies that
(\mathbf{t}_{2},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}^{\prime})
takes values in a known hyperellipsoid so that one may employ a more efficient algorithm.

The last function
Split(
\mathsf{constraint},p
)
excludes the solution
p
by splitting the assignment problem that satisfies
constraint
into several subproblems, which was originally proposed in Ref.
48
. It incorporates “excluding
p
” into
constraint
by returning
\tilde{Z}-1
mutually disjoint sets

\displaystyle\mathsf{constraint}

\displaystyle\cup\Big\{\overline{\big(1,p(1)\big)}\Big\},

(98)

\displaystyle\mathsf{constraint}

\displaystyle\cup\Big\{\big(1,p(1)\big);\overline{\big(2,p(2)\big)}\Big\},

(99)

\displaystyle\mathsf{constraint}

\displaystyle\cup\Big\{\big(1,p(1)\big),\big(2,p(2)\big);\overline{\big(3,p(3)\big)}\Big\},

(100)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\cdots\\
\mathsf{constraint}&\cup\Big\{\big(1,p(1)\big),\cdots,\big(\tilde{Z}-2,p(\tilde{Z}-2)\big);\\
&\qquad\overline{\big(\tilde{Z}-1,p(\tilde{Z}-1)\big)}\Big\}.\end{split}

(101)

This partitions all feasible solutions that satisfy
constraint
and are not
p
.
If a set contains incompatible requirements, such as
(i,j)
and
\overline{(i,j)}
, it will be discarded.
In Algorithm
2
, whenever we obtain a bottom PCT, at most
\tilde{Z}-1
sets like Eq. (
98
) are enqueued to
myconstraints
, each of which can yield a different bottom PCT via
OptimizePCT()
.

It should be noted that
mypcts
produced by Algorithm
2
is overcomplete—its elements are not noncongruent but rather appear in equivalence classes.
This is because for any
\mathbf{t}_{A}\in L_{A}
and
\mathbf{t}_{B}\in L_{B}
, we have
(+\mathbf{t}_{B})\circ\mathcal{J}\circ(-\mathbf{t}_{A})
congruent to
\mathcal{J}
, and their PCTs generally have different permutations unless
\mathbf{t}_{B}-S\mathbf{t}_{A}\in\tilde{L}_{B}
.
One may eliminate this redundancy by additionally invoking
Split()
to all “congruent” permutations, which is omitted here for brevity.
Another point to note is that a PCT generated by Algorithm
2
or
3
may represent a CSM whose SLM is not
(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
, but rather
(\tilde{L}_{A}^{\prime},\tilde{L}_{B}^{\prime},S)
, where
\tilde{L}_{A}^{\prime}\supset\tilde{L}_{A}
and
\tilde{L}_{B}^{\prime}\supset\tilde{L}_{B}
are finer lattices.
This can only happen when
S\tilde{L}_{A}=\tilde{L}_{B}
is a
proper
subset of
SL_{A}\cap L_{B}
, and is a direct consequence of the fact that the PCT representation does not forbid shuffles with shuffle lattices finer than
\tilde{L}_{B}
.
However, this feature is useful in practical applications (see Section
V.1
) and does not affect the completeness of the enumeration.

V
Application

In this section, we demonstrate the applications of Algorithms
1
–
3
through case studies of the B1–B2 and graphite-to-diamond transitions.
For implementation details, see our Python package
crystmatch

cod
, which utilizes
spglib
for symmetry detection
Atsushi Togo and Tanaka 2024
.
We will see that the enumeration range easily covers and goes beyond previously proposed SSPT mechanisms.
The resulting database can be further analyzed from multiple perspectives, or used as inputs to NEB-like methods.

V.1
Orientation relationship analysis

As one of the simplest SSPTs in compounds, the B1–B2 transition is similar to the FCC-to-BCC transition, but involves two atomic species.
Multiple distinct mechanisms—i.e., CSMs—have been proposed
Stokes et al. 2004
, including the purely distortive (
d=0
) Buerger one
Buerger 1951
, the Watanabe-Tokonami-Morimoto (WTM) one which can explain the observed orientation relationship (OR)
Watanabe et al. 1977
, and the Tolédano-Knorr-Ehm-Depmeier (TKED) one which is supported by MetaD simulations involving
{\sim}10^{4}
atoms
Tolédano et al. 2003
;
Badin and Martoňák 2021
.
Recently, the Therrien-Graf-Stevanović (TGS) mechanism was identified using the
p2ptrans
method
Therrien and Stevanović 2020
, which has a significantly lower strain.
These CSMs and their evidence are summarized in Table
2
.

A CSM can be decomposed into an SLM and a PCT, where the former consists of a deformation gradient
S
and a shuffle lattice.
Among these components, the
S
alone determines the OR and the habit plane, and can thus be compared directly with experimental observations.
In contrast, the shuffle lattice and the PCT describe microscopic details that are, currently, accessible only through simulations.
In this sense, listing all possible
S
is of broader interest, which can be achieved using Algorithm
1
.
To further characterize
S
from a CSM perspective, we used Algorithm
3
to compute the
minimal multiplicity
and
minimal shuffle distance
among all CSMs with deformation gradient
S
, denoted by
\mu_{0}(S)
and
d_{0}(S)
, respectively.
For simplicity, we let
\ell=2
and assign equal shuffle-distance weights to Cs and Cl atoms, i.e., set
\theta_{i}
as a constant.
A more physical choice of weights will be discussed in Section
V.2
.

Figure 9:

All deformation gradients
S
with
\mu_{0}\leq 6
and
\bar{\varepsilon}\leq 30\%
.
Each point represents a unique
S
, whose marker shape indicates its
\mu_{0}
.
(a) Each
S
is colored according to its estimated strain energy density
w(S)
.
Those
S
of previously proposed mechanisms are pointed out by gray solid lines, where the WTM one and TKED one share the same
S
, and the TGS one has the lowest
w
among all
S
enumerated.
(b)(c) Each
S
is colored according to its deviation angles
\varphi_{1}
from the WB OR and
\varphi_{2}
from the OFO OR.
Those
S
with strictly
\varphi_{1}=0
or
\varphi_{2}=0
are highlighted in blue.

Figure 10:
All deformation gradients
S
with
\mu_{0}\leq 12
and
w\leq 13\times 10^{8}\,\text{J}/\text{m}^{3}
.
Only the Buerger mechanism is omitted, as it has already been shown in Fig.
9
. (a) Each
S
is colored according to
\mu_{0}
, the minimal multiplicity required for an SLM to have
S
.
There are several
S
with
w
less than the TGS mechanism, but also higher
d_{0}
and
\mu_{0}
.
(b)(c) Each
S
is colored according to the deviation angles.
Blue crosses highlight those
S
with
\varphi_{1}=0
or
\varphi_{2}=0
.

As a preliminary investigation, we enumerated all SLMs for the B1–B2 transition with multiplicity
\mu\leq 6
(i.e., period
\tilde{Z}\leq 12
) and RMSS

\bar{\varepsilon}(S)=\sqrt{\frac{1}{3}\sum_{i=1}^{3}[\sigma_{i}(S)-1]^{2}}

(102)

less than 30%.
This was achieved by replacing
\Omega_{S_{0}}
in Algorithm
1
with
\{S\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}\,|\,\bar{\varepsilon}(S)\leq 0.3\}
.
From the enumeration result, we identified the SLMs of all established CSMs above, as shown in Fig.
9
(a).
A total of 788 noncongruent SLMs with 393 distinct deformation gradients are produced, taking 1 min 11 s on a single core of a Mac M1 chip.
The subsequent
d_{0}
calculation took only 13 s, producing 393 CSMs with the smallest
d
among all CSMs sharing their respective deformation gradients, i.e., satisfying

d(\mathcal{J})=d_{0}(S).

(103)

We refer to a CSM with this property as a
representative
CSM, whose PCT is necessarily a bottom PCT, though the converse does not hold.

Figure 11:

How the shuffle distance
d
depends on the weighting scheme, i.e., the ratio
\theta_{\text{Cs}}/\theta_{\text{Cl}}
for SSPTs in CsCl.
The figure shows 51 CSMs with the same SLM (
\mu=6
), whose
d
has only 8 distinct values when
\theta_{\text{Cs}}=\theta_{\text{Cl}}
.
Colors are used to distinguish between different CSMs.

It should be noted that all the 12 mechanisms in Table
2
are representative CSMs—except for the TKED one.
This means that Algorithms
1
and
3
are already capable of reproducing most known CSMs, while Algorithm
2
serves as a last resort for exhaustive enumeration.
One could certainly apply Algorithm
2
to every SLM, but a more efficient approach is to filter them using experimentally observed ORs.
As detailed in Ref.
27
, the most commonly observed ORs in the B1–B2 transition are the Watanabe-Blaschko (WB) OR

[001][110]_{\text{B1}}\parallel[110][001]_{\text{B2}},

(104)

and the Okai-Fujiwara-Onodera (OFO) OR

[100][111]_{\text{B1}}\parallel[111][100]_{\text{B2}}.

(105)

For both ORs, we computed the respective deviation angles
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
of each deformation gradient, defined as the minimal rotation required to produce the OR, as shown in Fig.
9
(b)(c).
We found that the deformation gradient of the WTM and TKED mechanisms is the only one that can produce the WB OR without rotation, which we denote by
S_{\text{W}}
.
On the other hand, the OFO OR can only be produced without rotation by two previously unreported deformation gradients, denoted as
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
.
At this point, we can see that
crystmatch
provides a systematic way to infer the deformation gradient—and thus the SLM—based on experimental observations.

One naturally wonders whether, as
\mu_{\text{max}}
increases, there exists a deformation gradient
S
with lower strain energy
w
than that of the TGS mechanism, or other
S
that can reproduce either OR without rotation.
The answer to both questions is affirmative as long as we set
\mu_{\text{max}}=12
, as shown in Fig.
10
.
Since the Buerger mechanism has
d=0
, we need not consider mechanisms with higher strain energy than it.
Accordingly, we set
w_{\text{max}}=13\times 10^{8}\,\mathrm{J}/\mathrm{m}^{3}
and performed a more comprehensive enumeration that took about 2 CPU hours, obtaining 9987 noncongruent SLMs and 5432 distinct deformation gradients.
Despite the total numbers increasing by more than an order of magnitude, only a handful of new cases emerged with
w
lower than that of the TGS mechanism,
\varphi_{1}=0
, or
\varphi_{2}=0
.

Figure 12:

All CSMs with
\mu\leq 6
,
w\leq 13\times 10^{8}\,\text{J}/\text{m}^{3}
, and
d\leq 1.8\,\text{\AA}
.
Only the Buerger CSM is ommitted, as it has already been shown in Fig.
9
.
Diamonds and crosses indicate representative and nonrepresentative CSMs, respectively.
Each CSM is colored according to its multiplicity.

V.2
Exhaustive enumeration

Now we assign shuffle-distance weights to Cs and Cl atoms.
If equal weights are used, a large number of CSMs will exhibit identical
d
values, as shown in Fig.
11
.
A physically reasonable approach is to consider the initial velocity
v
required to move from the initial state to the transition state on the PES within an average free time, which is proportional to the unweighted
d
.
Statistical mechanics tells us that heavier atoms are less likely to acquire velocities, since kinetic energy
T_{i}=\frac{1}{2}m_{i}v_{i}^{2}
.
Therefore, each atom’s contribution to
d^{2}
should be weighted as
\theta_{i}\propto m_{i}
, and
\ell=2
is thus an appropriate choice.
This leads to
\ln(\theta_{\text{Cs}}/\theta_{\text{Cl}})\approx 1.32
, which is sufficient to lift the degeneracy in
d
.

Figure 13:
All CSMs with
\mu\leq 6
and deformation gradient
S_{\text{W}}
.
CSMs that can be produced by Algorithm
2
using the same SLM are drawn in the same horizontal coordinate.
Yellow, green, and indigo represent
\mu=2,4,6
, respectively.
Gray lines point out the CSMs of the WTM and TKED mechanisms.
Note that CSMs with
\mu=2
also acquire PCT representations in SLMs with
\mu=4,6
.

To investigate the distribution of nonrepresentative CSMs and to reproduce the TKED mechanism, Algorithm
2
is applied to each SLM with
\mu\leq 6
and
w\leq 13\times 10^{8}\,\text{J}/\text{m}^{3}
, using
d_{\text{max}}=1.8\,\text{\AA}
.
This took about 3 hours, producing 320 757 CSMs, as shown in Fig.
12
.
However, if one is only interested in the CSMs that exactly conform to the WB OR, it is sufficient to apply Algorithm
2
only to those SLMs with
S_{\text{W}}
, taking merely 7 min 39 s.
Figure
13
shows all CSMs with
S_{\text{W}}
, from which one can see that while
S_{\text{W}}
belongs to the SLM of the WTM mechanism (
\mu=2
), it also belongs to 7 noncongruent SLMs with
\mu=4
and 13 noncongruent SLMs with
\mu=6
.
This is because for a given
(H_{A},H_{B},Q)\in\operatorname{IMT}(\mu)
, any
M\in\mathbb{Z}^{3\times 3}
with
\det M>0
induces an
(H_{A}^{\prime},H_{B}^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\in\operatorname{IMT}(\mu\det M)
with the same deformation gradient, where

\displaystyle H_{A}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{hnf}(H_{A}M),

(106)

\displaystyle H_{B}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{hnf}(H_{B}QM),

(107)

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}

\displaystyle=H_{B}^{\prime-1}H_{B}QH_{A}^{-1}H_{A}^{\prime}.

(108)

Overall, the total number of CSMs increases rapidly with both
\mu
and
d_{\text{max}}
.

Figure 14:

All deformation gradients
S
with
\mu_{0}\leq 6
and
w\leq 500\times 10^{8}\,\text{J}/\text{m}^{3}
.
Colors are used to distinguish between different multiplicities.
(a) The distribution of
w(S)
and
d_{0}(S)
at each pressure
P
.
Solid lines connect points representing the same SLM under different
P
, whose marker shapes change with increasing pressure, as indicated in the legend at the lower left.
(b) The lattice mismatch spectrum at each
P
.
Solid lines show the diverse behaviors of
w
as a function of
P
for different SLMs.

Note that
\mathcal{J}_{p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}}
satisfying Eq. (
59
) may have a period smaller than
\tilde{Z}
.
Consequently, the WTM CSM
\mathcal{J}_{\text{W}}
is reproduced in every
\operatorname{PCT}^{\star}(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S_{\text{W}})
such that
\tilde{L}_{B}
is a sublattice of the shuffle lattice of
\mathcal{J}_{\text{W}}\circ S_{\text{W}}^{-1}
; see Fig.
13
.
In fact, all CSMs exhibit this repetitive property under the PCT representation.
Although one could easily eliminate it by modifying Algorithm
2
and Definition
17
, doing so is unnecessary, since the current formalism ensures that
\mathrm{PCT}(\tilde{L}_{A},\tilde{L}_{B},S)
precisely consists of all possible CSM that a molecular-dynamics simulation employing periodic boundary conditions
\tilde{L}_{A}
(initial) and
\tilde{L}_{B}
(final), undergoing the deformation
S
, may produce—even when the resulting CSM possesses a smaller intrinsic period.

V.3
Effect of pressure on lattice mismatch

The densities of two solid phases of the same material can differ significantly, making their lattice mismatch and strain energy inevitably large.
In such cases, pressure plays a decisive role in the occurrence of SSPTs.
For example, at 1500 K, the graphite-diamond coexistence pressure estimated by the Berman-Simon line is
\sim
5 GPa, while the SSPT is not observed until
\sim
12 GPa
Bundy et al. 1961
;
Irifune et al. 2003
.
Previous studies also suggest that when the pressure is further increased to 20–50 GPa, both the critical nucleus size and nucleation energy decrease, as shown in Fig. 2 of Ref.
10
.
However, lattice mismatch cannot be quantified unless how lattices match is self-evident, or defined in advance—this is precisely what the concept of SLM provides.
By enumerating all possible deformation gradients, Algorithm
1
enables a comprehensive analysis of lattice mismatch.

We investigated the graphite-to-diamond transition under pressures
P=0,10,20,30,40,50\,\text{GPa}
.
The enumeration range was specified as
\mu_{\text{max}}=6
(i.e.,
\tilde{Z}\leq 24
) and
w_{\text{max}}=5\times 10^{10}\,\text{J}/\text{m}^{3}
to ensure inclusion of all established mechanisms, with the initial and final structures as well as elastic tensors calculated at each
P
.
The minimal multiplicity
\mu_{0}
and minimal shuffle distance
d_{0}
for each deformation gradient is also computed as we did in Section
V.1
.
For the case of
P=0
, enumerating the SLMs and computing
d_{0}
took 9 min 29 s and 37 s, respectively.
The joint distribution of
w
and
d_{0}
, as well as the spectrum of
w
are shown in Fig.
14
.
We can see that as pressure increases, the strain energy of those deformation gradients with small
d_{0}
or small
w
decreases significantly.
However, some other deformation gradients—particularly those with larger
\mu_{0}
and
d_{0}
—exhibit opposite or even nonmonotonic trends.
This suggests that the actual SSPT mechanism may vary under different pressures.
Overall, the “lattice mismatch spectrum” in Fig.
14
(b) extends leftward with increasing pressure, consistent with previous experimental and computational studies.

VI
Summary

We have introduced a cell-independent formalism that unequivocally defines the deformation gradient, SLM, and standard shuffle of a CSM, as well as the symmetry-induced congruence relations on
\operatorname{CSM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
and
\operatorname{SLM}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
.
This maps all possible CSMs of an SSPT onto a tree structure, which is finite up to congruence classes as long as the multiplicity, strain energy and shuffle distance are bounded.
Using the IMT and PCT representation, each CSM is encoded in a tuple of integer matrices with minimal redundancy.
Based on this, Algorithms
1
–
3
have been proposed for the exhaustive enumeration of CSMs and SLMs, unveiling novel SSPT mechanisms with unprecedentedly low strain or desired ORs.
They lay the groundwork for predicting CSM energy barriers via machine learning and can be seamlessly integrated with widely used methods with negligible computational cost.
One may also use them to study defect migration or heteroepitaxy
Wei et al. 2021
;
Li and Liu 2022
;
Li et al. 2025
.
All algorithms are implemented in the command-line tool
crystmatch
, which is open-sourced and freely available to the community
cod
.

We acknowledge helpful discussions with Xu-Yuan Zhou, Yuze Sun, Wei-Jian Jiang, Yi-Chi Zhang, Jia-Xi Zeng, Qi Liu, Yi Yang, Qing-Yang Zheng, and Jia-Cheng Gao.

The computational resources were provided by the supercomputer center at Peking University, China.

(109)

The outer loop of the above diagram means that
\mathbf{t}
is a translation element of
(+\mathbf{t}_{B})\circ\mathcal{J}\circ(-\mathbf{t}_{A})
.
The above derivation is reversible, so that the two CSMs have the same translation elements.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2
.

We prove the following proposition for each positive integer
n
by induction: If
L
is an addition group on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with rank
n
and

\lambda(L)=\inf_{\mathbf{t}\in L\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}}\lvert\mathbf{t}\rvert>0,

(110)

then

\exists\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{n},\quad L=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}\mathbf{t}_{i}\,\middle|\,k_{1},\cdots,k_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

(111)

Since Eq. (
110
) implies Eq. (
4
), Lemma
2
is nothing but a special case of this proposition for
n=3
.

Base case.
For
n=1
, let
\mathbf{t}_{1}
denote an element of
L
with length
\lambda(L)
.
Such an element must exist; otherwise,
L
would have infinitely many elements with bounded lengths, and hence a cluster point (by the Bolzano-Weierstrass theorem), which contradicts Eq. (
4
).
Since
L
is an addition group, we have
\{k_{1}\mathbf{t}_{1}\,|\,k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}\}\subset L
.
There are no other elements in
L
; otherwise, the minimum distance between elements in
L
would be smaller than
\lambda(L)
.

Induction step.
Now we show that for every
m\geq 1
, if Eq. (
111
) holds for
n=m
, it also holds for
n=m+1
.
Let
\mathbf{t}_{1}
denote an element of
L\subset\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
with length
\lambda(L)
.
Any element in
\mathbb{R}^{m+1}
can be uniquely decomposed as

\mathbf{v}=c_{1}\mathbf{t}_{1}+\mathbf{v}^{\prime},

(112)

where
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{v}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{t}_{1}\}^{\perp}
is orthogonal to
\mathbf{t}_{1}
.
This defines a projection mapping

\displaystyle\pi\colon\mathbb{R}^{m+1}

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathbf{t}_{1}\}^{\perp},

(113)

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbf{v}^{\prime}.

(114)

Since
\pi
is a linear mapping, the image of
L
under this mapping,
\pi(L)
, is an additive group with rank
m
.
In fact, we also have
\lambda(\pi(L))>0
; otherwise,
L
would have infinitely many elements with bounded lengths, and hence a cluster point.
Therefore,
\pi(L)
satisfies the proposition for
n=m
, so there must exist
\mathbf{t}_{2},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{m+1}\in L
whose images under
\pi
generate

\pi(L)=\left\{\sum_{i=2}^{m+1}k_{i}\pi(\mathbf{t}_{i})\,\middle|\,k_{2},\cdots,k_{m+1}\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}.

(115)

Also, since
L
is an addition group, we have

\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}k_{i}\mathbf{t}_{i}\,\middle|\,k_{1},\cdots,k_{m+1}\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}\subset L,

(116)

and we only need to show that there are no other elements in
L
.
Note that any
\mathbf{t}\in L
can be expressed as

\mathbf{t}=c_{1}\mathbf{t}_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{m+1}c_{i}\mathbf{t}_{i},

(117)

for some
c_{1},\cdots,c_{m+1}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Applying
\pi
to Eq. (
117
) and use Eq. (
115
), we can see that
c_{2},\cdots,c_{m+1}
must be integers.
This implies that

\big(c_{1}-\lfloor c_{1}\rfloor\big)\mathbf{t}_{1}\in L,

(118)

where
\lfloor c_{1}\rfloor
denotes the greatest integer less than or equal to
c_{1}
.
If
c_{1}\notin\mathbb{Z}
, the length of Eq. (
118
) would be nonzero and smaller than
\lambda(L)
, which contradicts Eq. (
110
).
At this point, we can see that all coefficients in Eq. (
117
) are integers, so that the proposition holds for
n=m+1
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3
.

If
\tilde{C}=CM
for some
M\in\mathbb{Z}^{3\times 3}
, then
\tilde{C}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})=C(M(\mathbb{Z}^{3}))
is a sublattice of
C(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
since
M(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
is a sublattice of
\mathbb{Z}^{3}
.
Conversely, if
\tilde{C}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
is a sublattice of
C(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
, there exist
\mathbf{k}_{1},\mathbf{k}_{2},\mathbf{k}_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}
such that the
i
-th column of
\tilde{C}
equals
C\mathbf{k}_{i}
, i.e.,
\tilde{C}=C[\mathbf{k}_{1},\mathbf{k}_{2},\mathbf{k}_{3}]
.
To see that the index of this sublattice is
\lvert\det M\rvert
, we only need to use the elementary divisor theorem
Cohen 2011
, which states that the quotient group
C(\mathbb{Z}^{3})/\tilde{C}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})
is isomorphic to a
\mathbb{Z}
-module of size
\lvert\det M\rvert
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4
.

Arbitrarily take
\mathbf{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{a}_{Z_{A}}\in\mathcal{A}
that are
L_{A}
-inequivalent, and
\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{k}\in L_{A}
that are
\tilde{L}_{A}
-inequivalent.
We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{Z_{A}}(\mathbf{a}_{i}+L_{A})

(119)

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{Z_{A}}\left(\mathbf{a}_{i}+\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{k}(\mathbf{t}_{j}+\tilde{L}_{A})\right)

(120)

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{Z_{A}}\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{k}\left((\mathbf{a}_{i}+\mathbf{t}_{j})+\tilde{L}_{A}\right),

(121)

where
\sqcup
denotes the disjoint union of sets.
Since each
(\mathbf{a}_{i}+\mathbf{t}_{j})+\tilde{L}_{A}
is a distinct
\tilde{L}_{A}
-equivalence class, we have
\lvert\mathcal{A}/\tilde{L}_{A}\rvert=kZ_{A}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5
.

Note that

\displaystyle C(\mathbb{Z}^{3})=C^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})\quad

\displaystyle\iff\quad C^{-1}C^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}^{3})=\mathbb{Z}^{3}

(122)

\displaystyle\iff\quad C^{-1}C^{\prime}\in\operatorname{GL}(3,\mathbb{Z}),

(123)

where we have used Eq. (
42
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
6
.

The existence of
H
and
Q
as well as the uniqueness of
H
are proved in Ref.
55
.
To see that
Q
is also unique, note that
Q=H^{-1}M
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
7
.

S^{\text{T}}S
is positive-definite, so it has three orthogonal eigenvectors
\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\mathbf{v}_{3}
(arranged in right-handed order) with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\lambda_{3}>0
.
Let
s_{i}=\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}
,
V=[\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\mathbf{v}_{3}]\in\operatorname{SO}(3)
, and
U=SV\Sigma^{-1}
.
We have
U\in\operatorname{SO}(3)
since
U^{\text{T}}U=I
and
\det U=1
.
Since
\lambda_{i}
is uniquely determined by
S
, so is
s_{i}
.
∎

Lemma 8.

When
\ell=2
, the right-hand side of Eq. (
70
) attains the minimum if and only if
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\overline{\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i}}-\overline{\tilde{\mathcal{J}}(\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i})}
, where the overline denotes the mean value

\overline{\mathbf{v}_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{Z}}\theta_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i}.

(124)

Consequently, a CSM with PCT
(p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}})
has

d(\mathcal{J}_{p,\mathbf{t}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{t}_{\tilde{Z}}})=\sqrt{\overline{\left|\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{p(i)}+\tilde{\mathbf{t}}_{i}-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i}-\overline{\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{j}}-\overline{\tilde{\mathbf{t}}_{j}}+\overline{\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{j}}\right|^{2}}},

(125)

where
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{i}=V\Sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}U^{\text{T}}\mathbf{b}_{i}
is deformed from the
L_{B}
-motif that defines the PCT, and so is
\tilde{\mathbf{t}}_{i}=V\Sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}U^{\text{T}}\mathbf{t}_{i}
.

Proof.

For any
\mathbf{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{v}_{\tilde{Z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\boldsymbol{\tau}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we have

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\overline{\lvert\mathbf{v}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\tau}\rvert^{2}}

(126)

\displaystyle=\overline{\lvert(\mathbf{v}_{i}-\overline{\mathbf{v}_{j}})+(\boldsymbol{\tau}+\overline{\mathbf{v}_{j}})\rvert^{2}}

(127)

\displaystyle=\overline{\lvert\mathbf{v}_{i}-\overline{\mathbf{v}_{j}}\rvert^{2}}+2\,\overline{(\mathbf{v}_{i}-\overline{\mathbf{v}_{j}})\cdot(\boldsymbol{\tau}+\overline{\mathbf{v}_{j}})}+\overline{\lvert\boldsymbol{\tau}+\overline{\mathbf{v}_{j}}\rvert^{2}}

(128)

\displaystyle=\overline{\lvert\mathbf{v}_{i}-\overline{\mathbf{v}_{j}}\rvert^{2}}+2\,\overline{(\mathbf{v}_{i}-\overline{\mathbf{v}_{j}})}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\tau}+\overline{\mathbf{v}_{j}})+\lvert\boldsymbol{\tau}+\overline{\mathbf{v}_{j}}\rvert^{2}

(129)

\displaystyle=\overline{\lvert\mathbf{v}_{i}-\overline{\mathbf{v}_{j}}\rvert^{2}}+\lvert\boldsymbol{\tau}+\overline{\mathbf{v}_{j}}\rvert^{2}.

(130)

We can see that
\overline{\lvert\mathbf{v}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\tau}\rvert^{2}}
attains its minimum
\overline{\lvert\mathbf{v}_{i}-\overline{\mathbf{v}_{j}}\rvert^{2}}
if and only if
\boldsymbol{\tau}=-\overline{\mathbf{v}_{i}}
.
Hence, when
\ell=2
, we have

d(\mathcal{J})=\sqrt{\overline{\left|\tilde{\mathcal{J}}(\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i})-\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{i}-\overline{\tilde{\mathcal{J}}(\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{j})}+\overline{\tilde{\mathbf{a}}_{j}}\right|^{2}}},

(131)

which is identical to Eq. (
125
).
∎

Lemma 9.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a crystal structure and
L
a sublattice of
\mathcal{A}
.
If we additionally require that
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
has no cluster points, then
\lvert\mathcal{A}/L\rvert
is finite.

Proof.

We assume by contradiction that the quotient set
\mathcal{A}/L
is infinite.
Take
\mathbf{t}_{1},\mathbf{t}_{2},\mathbf{t}_{3}\in L
that satisfy Eq. (
111
) and consider the parallelepiped

P=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{3}x_{i}\mathbf{t}_{i}\,\middle|\,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in[0,1)\right\}.

(132)

Any element in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
can be uniquely decomposed as

\mathbf{v}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}c_{i}\mathbf{t}_{i},

(133)

where
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Therefore, the mapping

\displaystyle\pi\colon\qquad\mathbb{R}^{3}

\displaystyle\to P,

(134)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{3}c_{i}\mathbf{t}_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{3}\big(c_{i}-\lfloor c_{i}\rfloor\big)\mathbf{t}_{i},

(135)

is well-defined.
Note that the image of each
\mathbf{a}+L\in\mathcal{A}/L
under
\pi
is a single element in
P\cap\mathcal{A}
, and

\pi(\mathbf{a}_{1}+L)=\pi(\mathbf{a}_{2}+L)\quad\implies\quad\mathbf{a}_{1}+L=\mathbf{a}_{2}+L,

(136)

i.e.,
\pi\colon\mathcal{A}/L\to P\cap\mathcal{A}
is injective.
Since
\mathcal{A}/L
is infinite, its image
\pi(\mathcal{A}/L)
is also infinite, and so is
P\cap\mathcal{A}
.
The Bolzano-Weierstrass theorem then states that
P\cap\mathcal{A}
must have a cluster point.
∎

Lemma 10.

Let
L
be a lattice, and
L_{1},L_{2}
its sublattice.
The intersection set
L_{1}\cap L_{2}
is full rank.

Proof.

Let
C,C_{1},C_{2}
be base matrices of
L,L_{1},L_{2}
, respectively.
We only need to prove that there exist nonsingular
N_{1},N_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3\times 3}
such that

C_{1}N_{1}=C_{2}N_{2}.

(137)

Lemma
3
states that
C_{1}=CM_{1}
and
C_{2}=CM_{2}
for some nonsingular
M_{1},M_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{3\times 3}
.
Therefore, Eq.
137
is equivalent to

N_{1}=M_{1}^{-1}M_{2}N_{2}.

(138)

Since
M_{1}^{-1}M_{2}
is rational, we can always let
m
be a common multiple of the denominators of its elements.
Then we have nonsingular
N_{1}=mM_{1}^{-1}M_{2}
and
N_{2}=mI
that satisfies Eq.
138
.
∎

Appendix B
Details of Algorithm
1

For empirical reasons, we generate
S_{0}\in\Omega_{S_{0}}
using the SVD
S_{0}=U\Sigma V^{\text{T}}
.
Here,
U
and
V
are independently, uniformly sampled in
\operatorname{SO}(3)
, while the generation of
\Sigma=\operatorname{diag}(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})
depends on
U
and
V
.
We sample
(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})
within the region
w(U\Sigma V^{\text{T}})\leq w_{\text{max}}
according to the distribution

f(s_{1},s_{2},s_{3})\propto\left\lvert s_{1}^{2}-s_{2}^{2}\right\rvert\left\lvert s_{2}^{2}-s_{3}^{2}\right\rvert\left\lvert s_{1}^{2}-s_{3}^{2}\right\rvert,

(139)

and then sort them to restore
s_{1}\geq s_{2}\geq s_{3}
.
This procedure ensures that the projection of
S_{0}
on
\operatorname{SO}(3)
, i.e.,
UV^{\text{T}}
, is uniformly distributed, while for each
(U,V)
, the conditional distribution of
S_{0}
follows the standard measure on
\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
.
The following algorithm analysis is based on this distribution, but one may well use other distributions to improve the performance of Algorithm
1
.

We begin by considering the probability that a given IMT
(H_{A},H_{B},Q)
is generated in a single iteration.
This can be estimated using the intersection between the cyan region and the pink hypercube centered at
Q
in Fig.
7
.
Given that strains in SSPTs are usually small, the intersection can be considered as a thin vicinity of the 3D section of Eq. (
89
) intercepted by
\operatorname{SO}(3)
.
Therefore, the probability of
(H_{A},H_{B},Q)
being generated is approximately equal to the probability that
\phi_{H_{A},H_{B}}(UV^{\text{T}})
is generated within this section.
Since the cyan region contains
Q
, the volume of this section can be assumed to be no less than 1.
On the other hand,
UV^{\text{T}}
is uniformly distributed over
\operatorname{SO}(3)
, whose volume as a 3D submanifold is
16\sqrt{2}\,\pi^{2}
.
Hence, after the deformation by
\phi_{H_{A},H_{B}}
, the probability density near
Q
is at least

\eta_{H_{A},H_{B}}=\frac{\sigma_{7}(\phi_{H_{A},H_{B}})\sigma_{8}(\phi_{H_{A},H_{B}})\sigma_{9}(\phi_{H_{A},H_{B}})}{16\sqrt{2}\,\pi^{2}},

(140)

where

\phi_{H_{A},H_{B}}=(H_{B}^{-1}C_{B}^{-1})\otimes(H_{A}^{\text{T}}C_{A}^{\text{T}})

(141)

is treated as a
9\times 9
matrix.

Now we consider the probability that no IMT in a given congruence class is generated in a single iteration.
Since each IMT corresponds to an SLM,
(H_{A}^{\prime},H_{B}^{\prime},Q^{\prime})
is congruent to
(H_{A},H_{B},Q)
if and only if there exist
R_{A}\in G_{A}^{\prime}
and
R_{B}\in G_{B}^{\prime}
such that

\displaystyle H_{A}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{hnf}(\hat{R}_{A}H_{A}),

(142)

\displaystyle H_{B}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{hnf}(\hat{R}_{B}H_{B}),

(143)

\displaystyle H_{B}^{\prime}Q^{\prime}H_{A}^{\prime-1}

\displaystyle=H_{B}QH_{A}^{-1},

(144)

where
\hat{R}_{A}=C_{A}^{-1}R_{A}C_{A}
and
\hat{R}_{B}=C_{B}^{-1}R_{B}C_{B}
are unimodular matrices that describe how
R_{A}
and
R_{B}
change the base matrices of
L_{A}
and
L_{B}
, respectively.
Therefore, the probability that no IMT congruent to
(H_{A},H_{B},Q)
is generated has a lower bound

\zeta_{H_{A},H_{B}}=\prod_{R_{A}\in G_{A}^{\prime}}\prod_{R_{B}\in G_{B}^{\prime}}(1-\eta_{H_{A}^{\prime},H_{B}^{\prime}}).

(145)

Replacing
(1-\eta)
in Eq. (
91
) with this expression yields a significantly smaller

i_{\text{th}}=\frac{\ln\epsilon}{\ln\displaystyle\max_{H_{A},H_{B}}\zeta_{H_{A},H_{B}}},

(146)

which can be computed numerically before Algorithm
1
starts.
